## Management Summary

### Verdict

🔴 **Critical security concerns** - This web shop lets attackers reach full server takeover, administrator accounts and customer data theft. It should run only as an isolated training target, never hold real customer data or payment cards, and never be exposed to the internet.

**Risk distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 4 · 🟠 High: 24 · 🟡 Medium: 27 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total: 55**<br/>**Findings register:** 103 · 🔴 Critical: 5 · 🟠 High: 46 · 🟡 Medium: 52 - the distribution above counts insecure-practice findings under their weakness; see [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register)<br/>**Assessment evidence:** 41 of 53 finding(s) confirmed in code<br/>**Weakness classes:** 37 (30 implementation, 7 design) - see [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register)

<br/>

**Security concerns behind this assessment:**

<blockquote style="border-left: 3px solid #dc2626; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 0;">

- **Server takeover through code execution** — A signed-in customer can run their own code on the server through a stored profile name (server-side code injection). *(🔴 [F-005](#f-005) → [W-001](#w-001))*
- **Customer data read and logins bypassed** — SQL injection lets anyone without an account read database tables and sign in without knowing a password. *(🔴 [F-004](#f-004), 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) → [W-002](#w-002))*
- **Anyone can become an administrator** — Mass assignment on registration lets an anonymous visitor create an account with the administrator role. *(🔴 [F-011](#f-011) → [W-003](#w-003))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain
- **Other customers' records exposed** — Missing ownership checks (IDOR) let any signed-in customer read and change other customers' records. *(🔴 [F-001](#f-001) → [W-007](#w-007))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain — left open by the evidence review: 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — Systemic missing ownership checks
- **Logins can be forged for any user** — A hard-coded token signing key in the source lets anyone with repository access forge a login for any account. *(🟠 [F-021](#f-021) → [W-010](#w-010))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain

</blockquote>

<br/>

Treat the system as unsafe for real users or data until the listed fixes are made and independently re-tested.

**About this assessment:** An AI-assisted threat model derived from the implementation of Juice Shop. It reconstructs the implemented architecture (9 components and 5 external services, see [§2](#2-architecture-diagrams)) and identifies threats and control gaps in it.


**Method and limits:** Automated static analysis of code and configuration, not a pentest or a team threat-modeling session; Low and Informational findings not reported (threshold: medium) - see [§11 Out of Scope](#11-out-of-scope).

### Remediation Priorities

The root causes behind the findings, ranked by severity, each with the measures to do first. The lasting fix under a root cause removes it and every finding it causes. Every mitigation that fixes a Critical finding is listed. Details in the [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register) and the [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register).

### Open Questions for the Team

The code cannot answer these questions; the people who own the business and the deployment can. Each question states under it what its answer changes.

- _How critical are Application records users, baskets, cards, addresses and sim, Alchemy API key and LLM API key to the business, and what harm would their disclosure, manipulation or unavailability cause?_
  The answer weights the impact rating and fix order of every finding that reaches these assets.
- _Which sensitive business records can the production database account used by Juice Shop Express API or Authentication & Session Surface access?_ ([W-002](#w-002): [F-004](#f-004), [F-010](#f-010))
  Personal or regulated data in reach raises both the breach impact and any notification duty; a narrowly scoped account limits the blast radius.
- _If an attacker runs code in Juice Shop Express API, which other services, secrets or credentials can that process reach?_ ([F-005](#f-005), [F-026](#f-026) (unproven))
  Every reachable service or credential extends this from one component to a wider compromise and raises the priority of isolating that process; an isolated process contains it.

### Security Posture & Top Threats

**Figure 1a - Runtime Architecture and Threat Overview**

At runtime, Juice Shop has 8 components in 3 layers and exchanges data with 5 external services. Figure 1a shows these data flows and where each of the 6 attack scenarios below begins. The complete boundary catalogue remains in [§1 Trust Boundaries](#trust-boundaries).

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-standard-v0.1.0.figure1-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-standard-v0.1.0.figure1.svg" alt="Figure 1a - Runtime Architecture and Threat Overview"></picture>

**Build pipeline**, not drawn in this runtime view: GitHub Actions, GitLab CI. ⑦ A2 · Supply-Chain / Build Attacker → [Figure 1b](#figure-1b).

Role access and finding assignments are listed in [Identified Actors](#identified-actors).

**Actor grouping.** Regular self-registered users are grouped with anonymous internet attackers because registration is open. Repository readers are grouped with anonymous internet attackers because the source repository is public. Each finding retains its login and privilege requirements.

<a id="figure-1b"></a>

**Figure 1b - Supply Chain and Build**

Juice Shop builds in GitHub Actions, GitLab CI. Figure 1b shows the inputs, build systems and release artifacts the repository evidences, with 8 findings attached to them. The highlighted path follows 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — Lockfiles disabled so every build resolves floating dependencies — .npmrc:1 as far as the repository evidences it. The control assessment is in [§6.11](#611-operations-runtime-and-supply-chain-controls).

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-standard-v0.1.0.figure1b-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-standard-v0.1.0.figure1b.svg" alt="Figure 1b - Supply Chain and Build"></picture>

**Figure 2 - Attack Routes and Impact**

Each numbered route names one example finding from the corresponding Top Threats group with its access prerequisite, the register weakness it is linked to, and the group's potential business harm, which depends on deployment and affected assets. W-IDs in parentheses link the example to an existing weakness. The attack step, affected component and technical consequence are listed in the findings register. Red arrows indicate attacks; dashed red arrows involve a victim; grey arrows lead to potential harm.

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-standard-v0.1.0.figure2-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-standard-v0.1.0.figure2.svg" alt="Figure 2 - Actors, attack routes, weaknesses and impact"></picture>

**Threat actors.** The actors below drive the numbered attack paths in the figures above. The **Juice Shop User** is the *victim* of client-side attacks (XSS / CSRF), not an attacker. Figure 2 marks victim interaction on the applicable attack routes.

- **Juice Shop User** — legitimate end user; target of client-side attacks; target of ⑥ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting.
- **Internet Attacker** — can self-register a regular account; drives ① Broken Authorization & Access Control, ② Insecure Query Construction & Data Access, ③ Remote Code Execution (unsafe eval), ④ Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography, ⑤ Sensitive File & Secret Exposure, ⑥ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting.
- **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** — compromises a dependency or build pipeline, or contributes malicious code to a public repo; drives ⑦ Build Pipeline & Dependency Integrity.

**7 structural threats**, grouped by weakness class - each row is one threat, not one finding. *Threat Description* states the general architectural weakness (STRIDE in brackets); *Findings* lists the concrete instances by severity under their component, each linked to the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register), and on screen an ID shows its title when you point at it; *Risk & Impact* combines severity with business consequence; *Primary Measures* names the most important measures for this threat, most important first, one per root cause. All measures are in the [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register).

| <a id="path-auth-bypass"></a>④ | **Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography** _(S·E)_<br/>A committed signing key and weak token verification allow session tokens to be forged for arbitrary users. | → [C-03](#c-03) Juice Shop Express API<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-014" title="JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist">F-014</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-013" title="Chat identity trusted from unverified JWT decode">F-013</a></span><br/>→ [C-08](#c-08) Authentication & Session Surface<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-017" title="Non-cryptographic RNG for a secret/token">F-017</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-021" title="Hardcoded JWT signing private key in source">F-021</a></span><br/>→ [C-01](#c-01) Sequelize application data access<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-043" title="Unsalted MD5 password hashing">F-043</a></span><br/><details><summary>3 Medium</summary>→ [C-08](#c-08) Authentication & Session Surface<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-070" title="JWT verification does not pin the signing algorithm">F-070</a></span><br/>→ [C-01](#c-01) Sequelize application data access<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-088" title="Hardcoded HMAC key for security answers">F-088</a></span><br/>→ [C-05](#c-05) frontend client application<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-101" title="Hardcoded test credentials in client bundle">F-101</a></span></details> | 🟠 High<br/>Full Admin Takeover · Customer Session Hijack | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-030](#m-030)</span> - Verify JWT signature before trusting identity<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-034](#m-034)</span> - Use cryptographically secure random values<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">● [M-036](#m-036)</span> - Load JWT private key from secret store |
| <a id="path-sensitive-data-exposure"></a>⑤ | **Sensitive File & Secret Exposure** _(I)_<br/>Unprotected file-serving routes and path handling expose keys, logs and server files to unauthenticated callers. | → [C-03](#c-03) Juice Shop Express API<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-024" title="Path Traversal">F-024</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-025" title="Zip slip arbitrary file write in complaint upload">F-025</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-044" title="Unauthenticated serving of keys, logs and quarantine">F-044</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-047" title="Server-side request forgery in profile">F-047</a></span><br/>→ [C-01](#c-01) Sequelize application data access<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-042" title="Payment card numbers stored in cleartext">F-042</a></span><br/><details><summary>5 Medium</summary>→ [C-03](#c-03) Juice Shop Express API<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-089" title="FTP file allow-list bypass">F-089</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-090" title="Null byte bypass of public file extension allowlist">F-090</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-091" title="Captcha answer returned in challenge response">F-091</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-092" title="Current user endpoint exposes fields">F-092</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-093" title="Unauthenticated memories endpoint returns joined user">F-093</a></span></details> | 🟠 High<br/>Customer Data Exfiltration | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-039](#m-039)</span> - Constrain file paths to a safe base directory<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-057](#m-057)</span> - Remove public serving of key, log and quarantine directories<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-060](#m-060)</span> - Validate and allowlist outbound request targets |
| <a id="path-cross-site-scripting"></a>⑥ | **Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting** _(T·I)_<br/>Unsafely rendered user content runs script in a victim's browser, where session tokens are readable. | → [C-05](#c-05) frontend client application<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-002" title="Session Token in Script-Readable Storage">F-002</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-037" title="Stored XSS">F-037</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-041" title="DOM XSS">F-041</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-038" title="Stored XSS">F-038</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-039" title="Stored XSS">F-039</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-040" title="DOM XSS">F-040</a></span><br/><details><summary>3 Medium</summary>→ [C-05](#c-05) frontend client application<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-086" title="Stored XSS">F-086</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-087" title="XSS">F-087</a></span><br/>→ [C-01](#c-01) Sequelize application data access<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-072" title="Product description stored without sanitization">F-072</a></span></details> | 🟠 High<br/>Customer Session Hijack | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-050](#m-050)</span> - Remove sanitizer bypass and encode feedback comments<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-024](#m-024)</span> - Store session tokens in HttpOnly, Secure cookies |
| <a id="path-supply-chain"></a>⑦ | **Build Pipeline & Dependency Integrity** _(T·E)_<br/>Unpinned dependencies and mutable build references let a compromised upstream package alter what is built.<br/>Path through the build: [Figure 1b](#figure-1b) · controls: [§6.11](#611-operations-runtime-and-supply-chain-controls) | → [C-02](#c-02) CI/CD Pipeline<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-022" title="Lockfiles disabled so every build resolves floating dependencies">F-022</a></span><br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-076" title="GitHub Actions referenced by mutable tag or branch">F-076</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-073" title="Missing Container Image Signing">F-073</a></span><br/><details><summary>5 Medium</summary>→ [C-02](#c-02) CI/CD Pipeline<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-074" title="Heroku CLI installed by piping remote script to shell">F-074</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-075" title="Untrusted npm Install/Postinstall Scripts Enabled">F-075</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-077" title="Container base images referenced by tag instead of digest">F-077</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-104" title="Container Runs as Root">F-104</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-105" title="Missing Workflow Permissions Block">F-105</a></span></details> | 🟠 High<br/>Full Server Compromise | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-083](#m-083)</span> - Pin third-party dependencies to immutable versions<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-037](#m-037)</span> - Commit lockfiles and install from them |

**Further threats without Critical or High findings:** CSRF / Permissive CORS (🟡 1). Their findings are in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

_STRIDE: S spoofing · T tampering · R repudiation · I information disclosure · D denial of service · E elevation of privilege. Risk, findings, components, impact and primary measures are derived deterministically; only the one-line weakness description is authored._

**Verified attack chains.** 9 fully viable ([AC-T-002](#ac-t-002), [AC-B-002](#ac-b-002), [AC-T-004](#ac-t-004), [AC-B-005](#ac-b-005), [AC-B-007](#ac-b-007), [AC-T-005](#ac-t-005), [AC-B-003](#ac-b-003), [AC-B-001](#ac-b-001), [AC-T-007](#ac-t-007)). These chains combine individual findings into end-to-end exploitation paths verified step-by-step against the code - see [§9 Abuse Cases](#9-abuse-cases) for the per-step breakdown and blocking mitigations.

### AI / LLM Exposure

This system embeds an LLM/AI surface (an LLM/AI integration); the risks below are architectural - they follow from how untrusted input reaches the model's prompt, tools, and outputs. See **[§6 Security Architecture](#6-security-architecture)** for the per-control detail.



- **[LLM01](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01/) Prompt Injection** — Untrusted user input reaches the LLM prompt/context without sufficient trust separation, letting an attacker override system instructions, redirect tool calls, or coerce unintended model behaviour. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-036](#f-036) — Client-supplied chat messages passed to model unvalidated _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-085](#f-085) — Stored product reviews fed to model as tool results _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-115](#f-115) — Chatbot coupon limits enforced only by prompt text

- **[LLM06](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm06/) Excessive Agency** — The LLM is granted tool/function-calling authority without a secondary authorization boundary, so a manipulated model turn can invoke privileged actions on the user's behalf. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Chat identity trusted from unverified JWT decode _([ASI03](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-062](#f-062) — Coupon tool discount policy enforced only in prompt _([ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-114](#f-114) — Chatbot coupon limits enforced only by prompt text _([ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-115](#f-115) — Chatbot coupon limits enforced only by prompt text

- **[LLM10](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm10/) Unbounded Consumption** — The LLM endpoint imposes no authentication, rate, or quota boundary, so any client can drive unbounded model invocations — uncontrolled provider cost and denial of service for legitimate users. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-048](#f-048) — Unauthenticated chat endpoint lacks consumption limits

- **[LLM05](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm05/) Improper Output Handling** — LLM-generated output flows into a downstream sink (browser, shell, query, eval) without validation or encoding, turning a model completion into an injection vector. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-114](#f-114) — Chatbot coupon limits enforced only by prompt text _([ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_

### Operational Strengths

Operational controls rated Adequate or Partial - grouped into broad clusters (full per-control breakdown in [§6](#6-security-architecture)). Clusters demoted to Weak by open Critical/High findings appear in [§6](#6-security-architecture) instead, not here.

<thead><tr><th>Strength</th><th>What's in Place</th><th>Effectiveness</th><th>Open gaps</th></tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Container &amp; Supply-Chain Hardening</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Build-time and runtime hardening - minimal base image, non-root execution, dependency inventory.</em><br/>Container image hardening and SBOM generation<br/>Dependency lockfile and install integrity</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">✅ Adequate</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">-</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Hardened HTTP Stack</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Browser-facing HTTP hardening — security headers, cookie flags, cross-origin policy, and abuse-protection limits.</em><br/>Socket.io CORS origin restriction<br/>Rate limiting</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">1 Medium finding remains open:<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-067">F-067</a> — OAuth implicit flow without state or nonce</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Observability &amp; Audit</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Runtime visibility - access logging, audit trails, and operational telemetry for post-incident review.</em><br/>WebSocket error logging and listener reset<br/>Access logging and exposure of logs/metrics</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">-</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>


**Bottom line:** These controls narrow specific attack surfaces but none eliminates a Critical finding on its own.

<a id="critical-attack-chain"></a><a id="critical-attack-tree"></a><a id="critical-findings-by-root-cause"></a>
## Critical Findings by Root Cause

5 of 5 Critical findings trace back to 4 root causes; fixing a root cause addresses every finding linked to it.

```mermaid
%%{init: {"flowchart": {"nodeSpacing": 14, "rankSpacing": 50, "padding": 8, "wrappingWidth": 260}}}%%
graph LR
    GOAL["5 Critical findings"]:::goal
    W001["W-001<br/>Application data crosses into executable code"]:::rootcause
    T005["F-005 - Server-side eval of stored username in profile page<br/>userProfile.ts"]:::leaf
    W002["W-002<br/>Database access relies on concatenated queries"]:::rootcause
    T004["F-004 - SQL injection in product search query<br/>search.ts"]:::leaf
    T010["F-010 - SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass<br/>login.ts"]:::leaf
    W003["W-003<br/>Privileged attributes lack an enforced write allowlist"]:::rootcause
    T011["F-011 - Registration accepts privileged role field from request body<br/>server.ts"]:::leaf
    W007["W-007<br/>Authorization is implemented route by route"]:::rootcause
    T001["F-001 - Systemic missing ownership checks<br/>memory.ts"]:::leaf

    W001 --> GOAL
    T005 --> W001
    W002 --> GOAL
    T004 --> W002
    T010 --> W002
```

**Structural fixes** (full detail in [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register)):

- **[W-001](#w-001)** — Replace evaluation of application values with a data parser or a fixed set of operations whose arguments remain data _(closes [F-005](#f-005))_
- **[W-002](#w-002)** — Provide one parameterised repository or query-builder path and prohibit application-value interpolation in database queries _(closes [F-004](#f-004), [F-010](#f-010))_
- **[W-003](#w-003)** — Define operation-specific write schemas and assign privileged attributes exclusively through server-side policy _(closes [F-011](#f-011))_
- **[W-007](#w-007)** — Enforce authorization through a shared server-side policy layer and make ownership and tenant scope mandatory inputs to data access _(closes [F-001](#f-001))_

---

## 1. System Overview

Probably the most modern and sophisticated insecure web application

**Repository:** `https://github.com/juice-shop/juice-shop.git`
**Runtime:** Node\.js 22 - 26

### Scope

This threat model covers 9 components of juice-shop: 

- Sequelize application data access
- CI/CD Pipeline
- Juice Shop Express API
- MarsDB Embedded Store
- frontend client application
- SQLite Embedded Store
- Web3 NFT and Wallet Routes
- Authentication & Session Surface
- Socket\.io Realtime Channel

**Not individually examined:** 134 source files in 5 components had no route, lead or security signal (Sequelize application data access 12, CI/CD Pipeline 4, Juice Shop Express API 3, MarsDB Embedded Store 4, frontend client application 111).

**Not analysed:** 92 TypeScript files the build leaves out, so their routes and code sites were not checked (`data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_1_correct.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_2.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_3.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_4.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_1_correct.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_2.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_3.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_4.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/changeProductChallenge_1.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/changeProductChallenge_2.ts` and 82 more).

**Not assessed:** 2 security-relevant code locations were read but could not be judged (Juice Shop Express API 1, frontend client application 1); `security-assessment.yaml` records each with its reason under `meta.undecided_targets`.

**Out of scope:** third-party hosted dependencies, browser runtime, operating-system kernel, and the underlying network infrastructure.

**Basis:** An automated, AI-assisted threat model built from the repository's source code and configuration - static analysis only, no dynamic or penetration testing. It describes the system as built, not as designed.

---

<a id="identified-actors"></a>
### Identified Actors

Figure 1a draws **Internet Attacker** as attacker and **Juice Shop User** and **Juice Shop Admin** as legitimate roles of Juice Shop. [Figure 1b](#figure-1b) draws **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** and the build it attacks. Each row gives that actor's access and the findings attributed to it.

| Actor | Type | Access | Findings |
|----------------------|---------------|----------------------|-----------------|
| A1 · Internet Attacker | Attacker | Can self-register a regular account | 🔴 5 · 🟠 44 · 🟡 43 |
| A2 · Supply-Chain / Build Attacker | Attacker | Compromises a dependency or build pipeline,<br/>or contributes malicious code to a public<br/>repo | 🟠 1 · 🟡 7 |
| Juice Shop User | Role (victim) | Person using the client application,<br/>evidenced by its browser entry point. | - |
| Juice Shop Admin | Privileged role | Application role with elevated rights. | - |

3 findings (1 High, 2 Medium) have no assigned actor. That does not mean they are out of reach.

---

### Trust Boundaries

Each row names a crossing and the condition it relies on. A modelled crossing does not prove that its control works.

<table style="table-layout:fixed;width:100%;max-width:1240px">
<colgroup><col width="20%" style="width:20%"><col width="25%" style="width:25%"><col width="21%" style="width:21%"><col width="34%" style="width:34%"></colgroup>
<thead><tr><th>Crossing</th><th>Required condition</th><th>Control</th><th>Status and findings</th></tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-1"></a>tb-1 · External → Juice Shop Express API</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Every state-changing or user-specific API route is registered behind <code>security.<wbr>isAuthorized()</code> before its handler runs.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><code>security.<wbr>isAuthorized()</code> route middleware</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Gap:</strong> <em>Validation</em><br/>🔴 <a href="#f-004">F-004</a> — SQL injection in product search query<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-032">F-032</a> — Request body can set UserId on generated create routes<br/><em>Authorization</em><br/>🟠 <a href="#f-003">F-003</a> — Missing object ownership checks on basket resources<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-046">F-046</a> — Any authenticated user can read any user record by id<br/><em>Not tied to one control</em><br/>🟠 <a href="#f-049">F-049</a> — Basket checkout lacks object ownership checks</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-2"></a>tb-2 · External → Authentication &amp; Session Surface</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Every state-changing auth/session route verifies a signed JWT or credential before acting.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><code>isAuthorized</code> <code>expressJwt</code> middleware</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Gap:</strong> <em>Validation</em><br/>🔴 <a href="#f-010">F-010</a> — SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass<br/><em>Authentication</em><br/>🟡 <a href="#f-068">F-068</a> — Wide TOTP window and weak second-factor throttling</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-6"></a>tb-6 · Juice Shop Express API → external</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">The server fetches only URLs that resolve to public image hosts, never internal or metadata addresses.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Not identified</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Gap:</strong> <em>Egress destination</em><br/>🟠 <a href="#f-047">F-047</a> — Server-side request forgery in profile</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-3"></a>tb-3 · External → CI/CD Pipeline</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Only reviewed code and SHA-pinned third-party actions execute in the pipeline on push and <code>pull_request</code> triggers.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">SHA-pinned action references</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Gap:</strong> <em>Validation</em><br/>🟡 <a href="#f-076">F-076</a> — GitHub Actions referenced by mutable tag or branch</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-7"></a>tb-7 · Frontend client application → external</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Only the intended Google OAuth endpoints receive the client id and access token.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Not identified</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Gap:</strong> <em>Egress content</em><br/>🟡 <a href="#f-100">F-100</a> — OAuth access token passed in URL query string</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-4"></a>tb-4 · External → Web3 NFT and Wallet Routes<br/>Crossing: inferred</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Web3 NFT routes only act for callers whose identity is established before the request is handled.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Not identified</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Unverified:</strong> 1 related finding</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-5"></a>tb-5 · Sequelize application data access → SQLite Embedded Store</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Every query reaches SQLite through Sequelize parameter binding, never through concatenated SQL text.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Sequelize parameter binding</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>No gap shown,</strong> control not confirmed</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-8"></a>tb-8 · Web3 NFT and Wallet Routes → external</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Contract events received from the Alchemy RPC are treated as untrusted before they solve challenges.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Not identified</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>No gap shown,</strong> control not confirmed</td></tr>
</tbody>

## 2. Architecture Diagrams

### 2.1 System Context

Who uses and attacks Juice Shop, and which external systems it exchanges data with. Solid arrows name the data a flow carries; dashed red arrows are attack routes. Actors carry the names Figure 1 uses (C4 Level 1).

## 3. Attack Walkthroughs

This section walks through how the highest-risk findings are exploited - one short walkthrough per Critical, each with attack steps, a focused sequence diagram, and the primary mitigation. Which root cause each Critical traces back to is in [Critical Findings by Root Cause](#critical-findings-by-root-cause); full per-finding context (severity rationale, assets, detection signals) is in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

### 3.1 Systemic missing ownership checks (IDOR) in Memory

**Source:** 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — `routes/memory.ts:15`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-001](#f-001) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. At `routes/memory.ts:15`, a signed-in attacker places another user's ID in the owner field of the request (`req.body.UserId` and the like) and the query trusts it instead of the session identity, so an attacker can read or change other users' records.
2. At `routes/basketItems.ts:68`, a logged-in user sends PUT `/api/BasketItems/``&lt;id&gt;` for an item in another user's basket; the item is found by id only and the owner is never compared, although `isAuthorized` and `appendUserId` authenticate the caller.
3. The result is horizontal privilege escalation across accounts.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - routes/memory.ts:15
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/memory.ts line 15
    App->>App: Systemic missing ownership checks
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-639
    alt Current state — F-001
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-001
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-023 — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (F-001)
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-023](#m-023) (Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (🔴 [F-001](#f-001))) lands, 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — Systemic missing ownership checks is exploitable at `routes/memory.ts:15` (Critical-severity, [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-023](#m-023) (Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (🔴 [F-001](#f-001)))
- Defence in depth: ◑ [M-001](#m-001) (Manual review: verify Systemic missing ownership checks in `routes/memory.ts:15`…)

### 3.2 Registration accepts privileged role field from request body

**Source:** 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — `server.ts:501`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-011](#f-011) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Send POST `/api/Users` with email, password, `passwordRepeat` and `role=admin`, with no credentials.
2. Log in with the new account and use the admin role on admin-only routes.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - server.ts:501
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting server.ts line 501
    App->>App: Registration accepts privileged role field from request body
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-915
    alt Current state — F-011
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-011
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-029 — Strip role from registration request bodies
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-029](#m-029) (Strip role from registration request bodies) lands, 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Registration accepts privileged role field from request body is exploitable at `server.ts:501` (Critical-severity, [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-029](#m-029) (Strip role from registration request bodies)

### 3.3 SQL injection in product search query

**Source:** 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — `routes/search.ts:23`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-004](#f-004) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An unauthenticated caller sends GET `/rest/products/search?q=` with a quote-closing UNION payload at `routes/search.ts:23`.
2. The q value is concatenated into a raw SQL string, so the caller can read other tables such as Users, including emails and password hashes, which come back in the JSON product list.
3. The only control is truncation of q to 200 characters, and the route has no authentication.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: Juice Shop Express API - routes/search.ts:23
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: GET /rest/products/search with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-004
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-004
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-026 — Use parameterized database queries (F-004)
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-026](#m-026) (Use parameterized database queries (🔴 [F-004](#f-004))) lands, 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — SQL injection in product search query is exploitable at `routes/search.ts:23` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-026](#m-026) (Use parameterized database queries (🔴 [F-004](#f-004)))

### 3.4 Server-side eval of stored username in profile page

**Source:** 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — `routes/userProfile.ts:61`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-95](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-005](#f-005) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Register or update my profile with username #{`process.mainModule.require('child_process').execSync('id')`}.
2. Log in and request GET `/profile` with my token cookie.
3. The server `eval()`s my code and I run arbitrary commands as the Node process user.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - routes/userProfile.ts:61
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/userProfile.ts line 61
    App->>App: Server-side eval of stored username in profile page
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-95
    alt Current state — F-005
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-005
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-027 — Remove eval of username
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-027](#m-027) (Remove eval of username) lands, 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Server-side eval of stored username in profile page is exploitable at `routes/userProfile.ts:61` (Critical-severity, [CWE-95](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-027](#m-027) (Remove eval of username)

### 3.5 SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass

**Source:** 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — `routes/login.ts:34`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-010](#f-010) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Send POST `/rest/user/login` with email set to a quote-terminated OR-true payload and any password.
2. Receive a signed JWT and basket id for the first matching user, typically the admin account.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: Authentication & Session Surface - routes/login.ts:34
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: Crafted HTTP request to the affected endpoint with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-010
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-010
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-028 — Use parameterized queries
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-028](#m-028) (Use parameterized queries) lands, 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass is exploitable at `routes/login.ts:34` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-028](#m-028) (Use parameterized queries)

### 3.6 Hardcoded JWT signing private key in source

**Source:** 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — `lib/insecurity.ts:21`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-021](#f-021) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request that reaches the code at `lib/insecurity.ts:21`.
2. The attacker sends it; the missing control never rejects the crafted input.
3. The `RS256` private key used to sign all session tokens is embedded in source; anyone with repository or build access can forge a token for any user or role such as admin or accounting.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Authentication & Session Surface - lib/insecurity.ts:21
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting lib/insecurity.ts line 21
    App->>App: Hardcoded JWT signing private key in source
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-798
    alt Current state — F-021
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-021
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-036 — Load JWT private key from secret store
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-036](#m-036) (Load JWT private key from secret store) lands, 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Hardcoded JWT signing private key in source is exploitable at `lib/insecurity.ts:21` (High-severity, [CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-036](#m-036) (Load JWT private key from secret store)

<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Application records (users, baskets, cards, addresses and similar)</strong><br/>Sequelize models persisted in the SQLite file data/juiceshop.sqlite.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Medium</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🔴&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-001">F-001</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-004">F-004</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-010">F-010</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-011">F-011</a></span><br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-003">F-003</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-032">F-032</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-046">F-046</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-049">F-049</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-050">F-050</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-051">F-051</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-055">F-055</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-056">F-056</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-057">F-057</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-058">F-058</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-059">F-059</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-061">F-061</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-063">F-063</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-064">F-064</a></span><br/><details><summary>12 Medium</summary>🟡 <a href="#f-081">F-081</a> - Feedback creation trusts UserId from request body<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-082">F-082</a> - Security answer creation accepts owner id from request body<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-096">F-096</a> - Order history filtered by lossy masked e-mail<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-097">F-097</a> - Unauthenticated order lookup by order id<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-106">F-106</a> - Deluxe role granted without payment<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-107">F-107</a> - Basket item add checks first BasketId but saves last<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-108">F-108</a> - Product review author taken from request<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-109">F-109</a> - Basket items readable and deletable by any authenticated user<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-110">F-110</a> - Basket item creation can target another user's basket<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-111">F-111</a> - Privacy request creation not bound to authenticated user<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-113">F-113</a> - Any logged-in user can delete any feedback<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-116">F-116</a> - Address update lacks ownership check</details></td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Alchemy API key</strong><br/>Credential embedded in the Alchemy WebSocket URL.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">High</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🔴&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-004">F-004</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-010">F-010</a></span><br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-037">F-037</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-038">F-038</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-039">F-039</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-040">F-040</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-041">F-041</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-043">F-043</a></span><br/><details><summary>4 Medium</summary>🟡 <a href="#f-068">F-068</a> - Wide TOTP window and weak second-factor throttling<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-072">F-072</a> - Product description stored without sanitization<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-086">F-086</a> - Stored XSS<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-087">F-087</a> - XSS</details></td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>LLM API key</strong><br/>Credential read from the environment for the LLM provider client.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">High</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🔴&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-004">F-004</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-010">F-010</a></span><br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-037">F-037</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-038">F-038</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-039">F-039</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-040">F-040</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-041">F-041</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-043">F-043</a></span><br/><details><summary>4 Medium</summary>🟡 <a href="#f-068">F-068</a> - Wide TOTP window and weak second-factor throttling<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-072">F-072</a> - Product description stored without sanitization<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-086">F-086</a> - Stored XSS<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-087">F-087</a> - XSS</details></td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Product reviews and orders</strong><br/>MarsDB collections 'posts' and 'orders'.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Low</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-033">F-033</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-034">F-034</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-050">F-050</a></span><br/><details><summary>5 Medium</summary>🟡 <a href="#f-065">F-065</a> - Product review author taken from client<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-083">F-083</a> - NoSQL operator injection<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-084">F-084</a> - Review update lacks ownership check<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-085">F-085</a> - Stored product reviews fed to model as tool results<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-108">F-108</a> - Product review author taken from request</details></td></tr>

</tbody>
</table>

*Sensitivity is assumed from the code, not set by your organization. No finding linked means that no finding is tied to this asset yet. It does not mean the asset is safe.*

_6 further entry point(s) in this category carry no linked finding and no elevated review signal, and are not listed individually (60 total). The complete route inventory is available in `.route-inventory.json` and, when exported, `pentest-tasks.yaml`._

---

## 6. Security Architecture

This chapter is organized by security-control category. Findings are listed only where the affected control is described.

_Cataloged controls: 41 total - 3 adequate, 17 partial, 13 weak, 3 unsafe, 5 missing._

**How to read the verdicts.** Every control category (and every sub-control below it) carries exactly one status. The two red verdicts do **not** mean the same thing - this is the distinction that decides what you have to do about a finding:

| Status | Meaning | What to do |
|----------|------------------------------------|----------------------|
| 🔴 Unsafe | Present and relied upon, but defeated or<br/>trivially bypassable | Fix the existing control |
| 🔴 Missing | Control was never built | Add the control |
| 🟠 Weak | Present, but has exploitable gaps | Strengthen it |
| 🟡 Partial | Present, but with meaningful gaps | Close the gap |
| 🟢 Adequate | Control is present and sound | Nothing, keep it |

So "🔴 Unsafe" on a control category does *not* mean the control is absent - it means the control exists but does not hold (e.g. an `MD5` password hash, a raw-SQL query path, a hardcoded signing key). "🔴 Missing" is reserved for controls that were never built (e.g. no `Content-Security-Policy` header).

### 6.2 Identity and Authentication Controls

<a id="ctrl-identity-and-authentication-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-004](#w-004), [W-011](#w-011), [W-012](#w-012), [W-013](#w-013)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe - login, registration and the supporting flows are present but defeated by SQL injection, client-controlled roles and weak recovery.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.2.1 OAuth implicit flow redirect URI allow-listing](#621-oauth-implicit-flow-redirect-uri-allow-listing)
- [6.2.2 Password change over GET with credentials in query string](#622-password-change-over-get-with-credentials-in-query-string)
- [6.2.3 Second factor (TOTP) and password reset](#623-second-factor-totp-and-password-reset)
- [6.2.4 User Registration](#624-user-registration)

**Implemented controls:** Database connection authentication, Cookie-based authentication on profile and image routes, OAuth implicit flow redirect URI allow-listing, Hardcoded test credentials in client, Password change over GET with credentials in query string.

**Assessment:** Authentication is built from a raw-SQL password login, open registration, a frontend OAuth adapter, optional TOTP and a security-question reset. The knowledge factor is broken at login (🔴 [F-010](#f-010)) and registration (🔴 [F-011](#f-011)). Each successful flow ends with the server issuing a session token; its signing, validation, storage and lifecycle are described in [§6.3 Session and Token Controls](#63-session-and-token-controls).

<a id="database-connection-authentication"></a>
**Database connection authentication.**


**Status:** 🟠 Weak - SQLite has no login of its own, so access depends on the app process and file permissions.

Sequelize connects to an embedded SQLite file using placeholder credentials. There is no database account to authenticate; only the Node process and the file system gate access.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Sequelize is configured with placeholder credentials for an embedded SQLite engine, which has no login of its own. Access control to the database depends entirely on the application process and on file-system permissions of the storage file. File permissions were not inspected. -->
Credentials carry no protective value here, and file permissions were not inspected.

- Placeholder username and password are ignored by the engine, so any process that can read the storage file reaches all data.
- Protection rests on host controls outside the inspected source.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="cookie-based-authentication-on-profile-and-image-routes"></a>
**Cookie-based authentication on profile and image routes.**


**Status:** 🟡 Partial - unknown tokens are rejected, but the cookie secret is hardcoded and CSRF protection is absent.

Profile and image routes read the `token` cookie and look it up in an in-memory session map. Requests with an unknown token are rejected before the handler runs.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The handler looks up an in-memory session map by the token cookie and rejects unknown tokens. The cookie parser uses a hardcoded secret, 'kekse'. No CSRF protection was observed on state-changing POST routes. -->
The lookup works, but the surrounding cookie handling is thin:

- The cookie parser signs with the hardcoded secret `kekse`, so signed cookies can be forged by anyone with the source.
- State-changing POST routes show no CSRF protection.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="oauth-implicit-flow-redirect-uri-allow-listing"></a>
#### 6.2.1 OAuth implicit flow redirect URI allow-listing

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the origin check works, but the implicit flow lacks state, nonce and audience validation.

`login.component.ts` enables Google login only when the current origin matches a configured authorized redirect. The access token returns in the URL fragment and is exchanged for a local session through `UserService.oauthLogin()`.

The diagram shows how the frontend OAuth adapter ends in the local login:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular OAuth Component
    participant Google as Google UserInfo API
    participant API as Local User and Login API
    User->>SPA: Return from Google with access token
    SPA->>Google: Fetch profile with token
    Google-->>SPA: Email address
    SPA->>API: Log in with derived local password
    API-->>SPA: Session token
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The client enables Google login only when the current origin matches a configured authorized redirect. It uses the implicit flow (response_type=token) with no state or nonce parameter, and the access token is passed in a URL query string to the userinfo call in user.service.ts. -->
OAuth acts as an identity hint, then converts into a local-password login:

- The implicit flow sends no state or nonce, so login can be forced or replayed (🟠 [F-015](#f-015), 🟡 [F-067](#f-067)).
- The local password is derived predictably from the email address (🟠 [F-016](#f-016)).
- The access token travels in a URL query string (🟡 [F-100](#f-100)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="hardcoded-test-credentials-in-client"></a>
**Hardcoded test credentials in client.**


**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - a test username and password ship inside the client bundle, readable by every visitor.

`login.component.ts` carries a prefilled test account in the Angular bundle that every browser downloads.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): A test username and password are embedded in the shipped client bundle. This is a credential exposure. -->
The credential exposure is a single weakness:

- Anyone who opens the bundle can try the embedded account against the login (🟡 [F-101](#f-101)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="password-change-over-get-with-credentials-in-query-string"></a>
#### 6.2.2 Password change over GET with credentials in query string

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - credentials in a GET query string leak through logs, history and referrers.

`user.service.ts` sends the current and new password as query parameters on a GET to the password-change route, which Express then handles in `routes/changePassword.ts`.

The diagram shows the intended password-change request path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular Client
    participant API as Change Password Route
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>SPA: Enter current and new password
    SPA->>API: GET change-password with query parameters
    API->>DB: Update stored hash
    API-->>SPA: Confirmation
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The current and new passwords are sent as URL query parameters on a GET request. They can leak through logs, history and referrers, and the request is not protected against CSRF. -->
Three weaknesses sit on this flow:

- Passwords appear in URLs and can leak to logs and referrers (🟡 [F-099](#f-099)).
- The current password is not mandatory, so a stolen session can change it (🟠 [F-029](#f-029)).
- Any non-empty new password is accepted (🟡 [F-066](#f-066)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="route-level-authentication-for-web3-listener-endpoints"></a>
**Route-level authentication for Web3 listener endpoints.**


**Status:** 🔴 Missing - Web3 listener handlers perform no authentication checks, so unauthenticated callers can trigger listener creation.

This control is not evidenced in the repository.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Handlers perform no authentication or caller checks within the module; any middleware applied at route registration in server.ts was not inspected. Unauthenticated callers can trigger creation of the shared WebSocket listener. -->
Unauthenticated callers can trigger creation of the shared WebSocket listener:

- Handlers in the Web3 module check no identity or caller, and any middleware at route registration was not inspected.
- Unbounded wallet address growth follows from the same open entry point (🟡 [F-103](#f-103)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="second-factor-totp-and-password-reset"></a>
#### 6.2.3 Second factor (TOTP) and password reset

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - TOTP checks bind to a typed temporary token, but the window is wide and throttling is weak.

`routes/2fa.ts` offers TOTP as an optional second factor: setup requires the password, and verification checks a typed temporary token. Password reset is a separate path that relies on a security-question answer.

The diagram shows the positive TOTP verification path after password login:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular Client
    participant API as 2FA Route
    User->>SPA: Enter TOTP code
    SPA->>API: Submit code with temporary token
    API->>API: Validate token type and code
    API-->>SPA: Session token
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): TOTP checks bind to a typed temporary token. The setup flow requires the password. The TOTP window is wide (epochTolerance 30). No rate limiting or lockout is visible in the inspected files. Password reset relies solely on a security-question answer with no throttling. -->
The second factor and recovery path both have gaps:

- The TOTP window is wide and no lockout is visible, so codes can be guessed (🟡 [F-068](#f-068)).
- Reset depends only on a security-question answer (🟠 [F-020](#f-020)).
- Reset sets no password strength policy (🟡 [F-071](#f-071)).
- The security-question endpoint reveals account details (🟡 [F-094](#f-094)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="user-registration"></a>
#### 6.2.4 User Registration

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - registration accepts a client-supplied role, so anyone can create a privileged account.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): Present at POST /api/Users -->
`POST /api/Users`, generated by the REST layer in `server.ts`, creates accounts straight from the request body. New users then log in through the normal password flow.

The diagram shows the intended registration path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular Client
    participant API as Users REST Route
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>SPA: Submit email and password
    SPA->>API: POST new user
    API->>DB: Insert user record
    API-->>SPA: Created user
```

**Security assessment**

Registration trusts the request body:

- A role field in the body is stored as given, so a new account can start as admin (🔴 [F-011](#f-011), 🟠 [F-063](#f-063)).
- The route is registered without authentication middleware logic limiting what fields are accepted (🟠 [F-056](#f-056)).



**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Registration accepts privileged role field from request body
- 🟠 [F-056](#f-056) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware
- 🟠 [F-063](#f-063) — Registration accepts client-supplied role

### 6.3 Session and Token Controls

<a id="ctrl-session-and-token-controls"></a>

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak - tokens are signed with `RS256` and expire, but the key is public, storage is script-readable and revocation is absent.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.3.1 Client-side session token storage](#631-client-side-session-token-storage)
- [6.3.2 Session Token Issuance](#632-session-token-issuance)

**Implemented controls:** Client-side session token storage, Session Token Issuance (JWT Based).

**Assessment:** This application uses a single locally-signed JWT for every session, whichever [§6.2](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls) flow created it. The sub-sections trace issuance and browser storage; the token is script-readable (🟠 [F-002](#f-002)), its key is hardcoded (🟠 [F-021](#f-021)), and no revocation exists.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-002](#f-002) — Session Token in Script-Readable Storage

<a id="client-side-session-token-storage"></a>
#### 6.3.1 Client-side session token storage

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the JWT sits in `localStorage` and a script-set cookie, so any XSS reads the session.

The Angular client keeps the JWT in `localStorage` and also writes it to a JavaScript-set cookie with an 8-hour expiry. Requests attach it from there.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The JWT is stored in localStorage and in a JavaScript-set cookie (put with an 8-hour expiry, no HttpOnly or Secure option visible). Any script running in the page can read it, so an XSS flaw exposes the session. -->
Storage is readable by page scripts:

- No `HttpOnly` or `Secure` option is visible on the cookie, so script-readable storage exposes the session (🟠 [F-002](#f-002)).
- The many XSS findings in [§6.7](#67-output-encoding-and-rendering-controls) therefore convert directly to account takeover.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="session-token-issuance"></a><a id="session-token-issuance-jwt-based"></a>
#### 6.3.2 Session Token Issuance

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - `RS256` and a 6h expiry are in place, but the key is public and the algorithm is not pinned.

`lib/insecurity.ts` signs a JWT with `RS256` on login, valid for 6 hours, and keeps issued tokens in an in-memory map.

The diagram shows token issuance after a successful login:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Login Route
    participant Sec as Token Signer
    User->>API: Credentials
    API->>Sec: Sign user record with private key
    Sec-->>API: JWT
    API-->>User: Token and basket id
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Tokens are signed with RS256 and expire after 6h. The private key is embedded in source. The full user record, including the password hash, goes into the token payload. verify() uses jws.verify without pinning the algorithm. Tokens live in an in-memory map with no revocation. -->
Issuance and validation each carry defects:

- The RSA private key is committed to source, so anyone can mint tokens (🟠 [F-021](#f-021)).
- Verification does not pin the algorithm (🟠 [F-014](#f-014), 🟡 [F-070](#f-070)).
- The password hash is placed inside the token payload (🟡 [F-102](#f-102)).
- Role checks skip expiry enforcement (🟡 [F-069](#f-069)).
- The in-memory map gives no revocation.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.4 Authorization Controls

<a id="ctrl-authorization-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-003](#w-003), [W-007](#w-007), [W-022](#w-022), [W-023](#w-023), [W-024](#w-024), [W-025](#w-025)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak - middleware guards many routes, but coverage is uneven and object ownership is rarely checked.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.4.1 Route-level authorization middleware in server.ts](#641-route-level-authorization-middleware-in-serverts)
- [6.4.2 Per-object ownership check in the chatbot getOrderById tool](#642-per-object-ownership-check-in-the-chatbot-getorderbyid-tool)
- [6.4.3 Role checks and user binding](#643-role-checks-and-user-binding)

**Implemented controls:** Route-level authorization middleware in `server.ts`, Per-object ownership check in the chatbot `getOrderById` tool, Role checks and user binding.

**Assessment:** Authorization relies on per-route middleware in `server.ts` with no shared server-side pattern. Gaps span unguarded routes (🟠 [F-061](#f-061), 🟠 [F-056](#f-056)), missing ownership checks (🔴 [F-001](#f-001)) and client-supplied identifiers (🟠 [F-032](#f-032)).

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — Systemic missing ownership checks
- 🟠 [F-003](#f-003) — Missing object ownership checks on basket resources
- 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Registration accepts privileged role field from request body
- 🟠 [F-032](#f-032) — Request body can set UserId on generated create routes
- 🟠 [F-046](#f-046) — Any authenticated user can read any user record by id
- 🟠 [F-049](#f-049) — Basket checkout lacks object ownership checks
- 🟠 [F-050](#f-050) — Any user can edit other users' product
- 🟠 [F-051](#f-051) — Recycle records readable by any caller by id
- 🟠 [F-055](#f-055) — Key, log and quarantine files served without authorization
- 🟠 [F-056](#f-056) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware
- 🟠 [F-057](#f-057) — Any logged-in user can list all user accounts
- 🟠 [F-058](#f-058) — Any logged-in user can create products
- 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — Any logged-in user can list all complaints
- 🟠 [F-061](#f-061) — Unauthenticated product update
- 🟠 [F-062](#f-062) — Coupon tool discount policy enforced only in prompt
- 🟠 [F-063](#f-063) — Registration accepts client-supplied role
- 🟠 [F-064](#f-064) — Missing role check on user list endpoint
- 🟡 [F-081](#f-081) — Feedback creation trusts UserId from request body
- 🟡 [F-082](#f-082) — Security answer creation accepts owner id from request body

- 🟡 [F-097](#f-097) — Unauthenticated order lookup by order id
- 🟡 [F-098](#f-098) — Order lookup by lossy vowel-masked e-mail in data
- 🟡 [F-106](#f-106) — Deluxe role granted without payment
- 🟡 [F-107](#f-107) — Basket item add checks first BasketId but saves last
- 🟡 [F-108](#f-108) — Product review author taken from request
- 🟡 [F-109](#f-109) — Basket items readable and deletable by any authenticated user
- 🟡 [F-110](#f-110) — Basket item creation can target another user's basket
- 🟡 [F-111](#f-111) — Privacy request creation not bound to authenticated user
- 🟡 [F-113](#f-113) — Any logged-in user can delete any feedback
- 🟡 [F-116](#f-116) — Address update lacks ownership check

<a id="route-level-authorization-middleware-in-serverts"></a>
#### 6.4.1 Route-level authorization middleware in server.ts

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - `isAuthorized`, `denyAll` and `isAccounting` guard many routes, but coverage has holes.

`server.ts` attaches `isAuthorized`, `denyAll` and `isAccounting` per route to protect much of the REST and API surface.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): isAuthorized, denyAll and isAccounting guard many routes. Coverage is uneven. PUT /api/Products is not guarded (the line is commented out), and /rest/chat, /rest/memories and PUT /rest/products/:id/reviews have no isAuthorized middleware at the route. The accounting IP filter uses a malformed placeholder address. -->
Coverage is uneven:

- `PUT /api/Products` has its guard commented out (🟠 [F-061](#f-061)).
- Several routes register with no authentication middleware (🟠 [F-056](#f-056)).
- Any logged-in user can list accounts, complaints and create products (🟠 [F-057](#f-057), 🟠 [F-059](#f-059), 🟠 [F-058](#f-058)).
- Any logged-in user can delete feedback (🟡 [F-113](#f-113)).
- Key, log and quarantine files are served openly (🟠 [F-055](#f-055)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="per-object-ownership-check-in-the-chatbot-getorderbyid-tool"></a>
#### 6.4.2 Per-object ownership check in the chatbot getOrderById tool

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the tool authenticates the user, but compares a lossy vowel-masked email.

The chatbot `getOrderById` tool in `routes/chat.ts` authenticates the caller and filters orders by the user's masked email address.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The tool authenticates the user and compares the order email with a vowel-masked user email. The masking is lossy, so different emails can collide. Inspected scope: the chat.ts tool definitions and the system prompt. Tool calls are model-directed and need no approval step. -->
The ownership test is imprecise:

- Vowel masking is lossy, so different emails collide and can read each other's orders (🟡 [F-096](#f-096), 🟡 [F-098](#f-098)).
- Tool calls are model-directed with no approval step.
- Order tracking by id needs no login at all (🟡 [F-097](#f-097)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="role-checks-and-user-binding"></a>
#### 6.4.3 Role checks and user binding

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - role claims are verified per route, so coverage depends on each route's wiring.

`isAccounting`, `isDeluxe` and `isCustomer` verify the token signature and read the role claim. `appendUserId` binds `UserId` from the server-side token map.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): isAccounting, isDeluxe and isCustomer verify the signature and check the role claim. appendUserId binds UserId from the server-side token map. The checks are applied per route, so coverage depends on how routes use them. -->
Role checks help only where applied:

- Ownership is missing across baskets, orders and records (🔴 [F-001](#f-001), 🟠 [F-003](#f-003), 🟠 [F-049](#f-049)).
- The user list lacks a role check (🟠 [F-064](#f-064)).
- Deluxe status is granted without payment (🟡 [F-106](#f-106)).

### 6.5 Query Construction and Data Access Controls

<a id="ctrl-query-construction-and-data-access-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-002](#w-002), [W-006](#w-006)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe - raw SQL and string-built NoSQL selectors defeat the query layer on the login and search routes.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.5.1 Transaction mode and busy-retry configuration](#651-transaction-mode-and-busy-retry-configuration)
- [6.5.2 NoSQL query construction in chatbot tools](#652-nosql-query-construction-in-chatbot-tools)
- [6.5.3 Login query construction](#653-login-query-construction)

**Implemented controls:** Transaction mode and busy-retry configuration, NoSQL query construction in chatbot tools, Login query construction.

**Assessment:** Sequelize models back most access, yet login and search call raw SQL (🔴 [F-010](#f-010), 🔴 [F-004](#f-004)) and MarsDB selectors take user-shaped input (🟠 [F-033](#f-033), 🟠 [F-034](#f-034) (NoSQL JavaScript injection in order tracking \$where)). Transaction settings do not offset this.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — SQL injection in product search query
- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass
- 🟠 [F-033](#f-033) — NoSQL JavaScript injection in product
- 🟠 [F-034](#f-034) — NoSQL JavaScript injection in order tracking \$where
- 🟡 [F-083](#f-083) — NoSQL operator injection
- 🟡 [F-084](#f-084) — Review update lacks ownership check

<a id="transaction-mode-and-busy-retry-configuration"></a>
#### 6.5.1 Transaction mode and busy-retry configuration

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - IMMEDIATE transactions and busy retries help consistency, not input safety.

Sequelize runs transactions in IMMEDIATE mode and retries `SQLITE_BUSY` failures up to 5 times.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Transactions use IMMEDIATE mode, and queries that fail with SQLITE_BUSY are retried up to 5 times. This helps write consistency and availability. It does not provide authorization or input safety. Query construction in the model files and routes was not inspected in this slice. -->
This setting covers write consistency only:

- It adds no authorization or input handling.
- Query construction in models and routes is assessed in the other sub-sections.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="nosql-query-construction-in-chatbot-tools"></a>
#### 6.5.2 NoSQL query construction in chatbot tools

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - one tool is parameterized, but the review lookup builds a `$where` string.

Chatbot tools in `routes/chat.ts` use parameterized Sequelize operators in `searchProducts`, while `getProductReviews` builds a MarsDB `$where` string from `Number(id)`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): getProductReviews builds a \$where string by concatenating Number(id). A non-numeric id becomes NaN, which limits injection. The searchProducts tool uses parameterized Sequelize operators. -->
The chatbot tools are comparatively tight, the review routes are not:

- `Number(id)` turns non-numeric input into NaN, which limits that tool.
- The same selector pattern is injectable elsewhere (🟠 [F-033](#f-033), 🟠 [F-034](#f-034) — NoSQL JavaScript injection in order tracking \$where (NoSQL JavaScript injection in order tracking \$where), 🟡 [F-083](#f-083) — NoSQL operator injection).
- Review updates lack an ownership check (🟡 [F-084](#f-084)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="login-query-construction"></a>
#### 6.5.3 Login query construction

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the email is interpolated into raw SQL, so the credential check is bypassed.

`routes/login.ts` `MD5`-hashes the submitted password, then builds the user lookup as a raw SQL string with `req.body.email` inside it.

The diagram shows the intended login lookup:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Login Route
    participant DB as SQLite Users Table
    User->>API: Email and password
    API->>DB: Query user by email and password hash
    DB-->>API: Matching user row
    API-->>User: Session token
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The login query interpolates req.body.email directly into a raw SQL string. The password is MD5-hashed first, but the email is not parameterized. This allows authentication bypass, so the credential check is defeated. -->
Only the password is processed before the query:

- The email is not parameterized, so a crafted value logs in as the first row, usually admin (🔴 [F-010](#f-010)).
- Product search repeats the pattern (🔴 [F-004](#f-004)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.6 Input Boundary Validation Controls

<a id="ctrl-input-boundary-validation-controls"></a>

**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing - validation is ad hoc per route, with no shared schema, and key inputs go unchecked.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.6.1 Validation Approach](#661-validation-approach)
- [6.6.2 Upload size and type limits](#662-upload-size-and-type-limits)
- [6.6.3 Validation of walletAddress request input](#663-validation-of-walletaddress-request-input)
