- [6.6.5 Socket event payload validation](#665-socket-event-payload-validation)

**Implemented controls:** Upload size and type limits, Redirect allowlist and sanitization, Socket event payload validation.

**Assessment:** Upload limits exist, but request bodies have no schema validation. Wallet amounts (🟠 [F-023](#f-023)), wallet addresses (🟡 [F-103](#f-103)) and chat traffic (🟠 [F-048](#f-048)) pass through unchecked.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-023](#f-023) — Wallet top-up accepts arbitrary client balance amount
- 🟠 [F-048](#f-048) — Unauthenticated chat endpoint lacks consumption limits
- 🟡 [F-103](#f-103) — Unbounded wallet address set growth on unauthenticated
- 🟡 [F-114](#f-114) — Chatbot coupon limits enforced only by prompt text

<a id="validation-approach"></a>
#### 6.6.1 Validation Approach

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - each route validates for itself, and no schema layer exists.

Validation is written inside individual route handlers and event handlers. No shared schema library or request validator was evidenced.

**Security assessment**

Missing shared validation leaves business inputs open:

- Wallet top-up accepts any client amount (🟠 [F-023](#f-023)).
- The chat endpoint has no consumption limits (🟠 [F-048](#f-048)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="upload-size-and-type-limits"></a>
#### 6.6.2 Upload size and type limits

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - size and type caps exist, but the MIME type is client-declared.

Memory uploads cap at 200 KB, and the disk upload accepts only png and jpeg MIME types. Filenames pass through `sanitizeFilename`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Memory uploads are capped at 200 KB, and the disk upload accepts only the png and jpeg MIME types. The MIME type is client-declared. The filename is sanitized with sanitizeFilename. -->
The caps help against casual abuse:

- The MIME type comes from the client and is not content-checked.
- Archive extraction still allows path escape (🟠 [F-025](#f-025)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="validation-of-walletaddress-request-input"></a>
#### 6.6.3 Validation of walletAddress request input

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - `walletAddress` is stored without any type, format or length check.

This control is not evidenced in the repository.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): req.body.walletAddress is added to an in-memory Set or looked up without type, format or length validation; unbounded additions to walletsConnected permit memory growth. -->
`req.body.walletAddress` goes into an in-memory Set or lookup with no checks:

- Unbounded additions grow `walletsConnected` and exhaust memory (🟡 [F-103](#f-103)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="redirect-allowlist-and-sanitization"></a>
#### 6.6.4 Redirect allowlist and sanitization

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the allowlist uses substring matching that can be bypassed.

Redirect targets are checked against an allowlist using `url.includes`. `saveLoginIp` sanitizes the header iteratively when challenge mode is off.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The redirect allowlist uses substring matching with url.includes, which can be bypassed. saveLoginIp sanitizes the header iteratively, but only when the challenge mode is off. -->
Substring matching does not pin the destination:

- A hostile URL containing an allowed string passes.
- Header sanitization is skipped in challenge mode.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="socket-event-payload-validation"></a>
#### 6.6.5 Socket event payload validation

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - event payloads are typed `any` with no schema checks.

Socket\.io handlers receive `any` payloads and run ad-hoc checks: substring matching, a regex with `isRedirectAllowed`, and `Array.isArray`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Event payloads are typed 'any' and have no schema validation. The handlers only do ad-hoc checks, such as substring matching, a regex with an isRedirectAllowed check, and an Array.isArray check. These checks decide whether a challenge counts as solved. They are not security validation. The only rate limiting or size limiting in view is the Socket.io default. -->
These checks decide challenge state, not security:

- No schema, size limit or rate limit exists beyond Socket\.io defaults.
- Handlers act on whatever shape arrives.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.9 Cryptography Secrets and Data Protection


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-036](#w-036)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak - secrets are committed to source and passwords use fast unsalted hashing.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.9.1 LLM API key handling and startup model probe](#691-llm-api-key-handling-and-startup-model-probe)
- [6.9.2 Transport protection for API calls](#692-transport-protection-for-api-calls)
- [6.9.3 Database storage protection](#693-database-storage-protection)
- [6.9.4 Alchemy API key handling](#694-alchemy-api-key-handling)
- [6.9.5 Password hashing and security-answer HMAC](#695-password-hashing-and-security-answer-hmac)

**Implemented controls:** LLM API key handling and startup model probe, Transport protection for API calls, Database storage protection (encryption at rest and engine credentials), Alchemy API key handling, Password hashing and security-answer HMAC.

**Assessment:** Keys and secrets sit in source (🟠 [F-021](#f-021), 🟡 [F-088](#f-088), 🟡 [F-101](#f-101)), tokens come from a non-cryptographic RNG (🟠 [F-017](#f-017)), cards are stored in cleartext (🟠 [F-042](#f-042)) and passwords use `MD5` (🟠 [F-043](#f-043) (Unsalted `MD5` password hashing)). Environment variables hold only the LLM and Alchemy keys.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — Non-cryptographic RNG for a secret/token
- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Hardcoded JWT signing private key in source
- 🟠 [F-042](#f-042) — Payment card numbers stored in cleartext
- 🟡 [F-088](#f-088) — Hardcoded HMAC key for security answers
- 🟡 [F-101](#f-101) — Hardcoded test credentials in client bundle

<a id="llm-api-key-handling-and-startup-model-probe"></a>
#### 6.9.1 LLM API key handling and startup model probe

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - key comes from an environment variable and the probe sends no key.

The LLM key is read from `LLM_API_KEY`. The URL is operator-configured, and the startup probe uses a 5-second timeout without sending the key.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The API key is read from the LLM_API_KEY environment variable, not hardcoded. The URL is operator-configured. The startup probe uses a 5-second timeout and sends no key. Transport security depends on the configured URL scheme. -->
Sourcing is sound, transport is open:

- Transport security depends on the configured URL scheme.
- No finding is routed here.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="transport-protection-for-api-calls"></a>
#### 6.9.2 Transport protection for API calls

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - API calls are modelled as HTTP with no TLS enforcement in source.

Angular services build API URLs from `environment.hostServer`, covering logins, payment cards and the private-key submission in `keys.service.ts`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The services build API URLs from environment.hostServer. The flows are modelled as HTTP, and the source I read does not enforce TLS. Payment cards, login credentials and the private-key submission in keys.service.ts travel over these calls. Production TLS termination was not inspected. -->
TLS is not enforced in what was read:

- Credentials and card data travel over these calls.
- Production TLS termination was not inspected, so deployment may differ.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="database-storage-protection"></a><a id="database-storage-protection-encryption-at-rest-and-engine-credentials"></a>
#### 6.9.3 Database storage protection

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - a plain SQLite file with no encryption option configured.

Sequelize writes to `data/juiceshop.sqlite` with placeholder credentials that SQLite ignores.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The Sequelize instance uses placeholder credentials ('database','username','password'), which SQLite ignores. The store is a plain file at data/juiceshop.sqlite with no encryption option configured in this file. Protection depends on filesystem permissions and host controls, which were not inspected here. -->
Data at rest depends on the host:

- No encryption option is configured in this file.
- Payment card numbers are stored in cleartext in the database (🟠 [F-042](#f-042)).
- Filesystem permissions were not inspected.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="alchemy-api-key-handling"></a>
#### 6.9.4 Alchemy API key handling

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the key is read from the environment and the socket uses `wss`, but it is embedded in the URL.

The Alchemy key is read from an environment variable and used to build a `wss` provider URL.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The key is read from an environment variable rather than hardcoded, and the connection uses wss. It falls back to an empty string silently, and the key is embedded in the URL. -->
Handling is better than hardcoding, with two gaps:

- The key falls back silently to an empty string.
- It is embedded in the URL, so URL logging exposes it.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="password-hashing-and-security-answer-hmac"></a>
#### 6.9.5 Password hashing and security-answer HMAC

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - passwords use unsalted `MD5` and the answer HMAC key is hardcoded.

`lib/insecurity.ts` hashes passwords with `MD5`. `models/securityAnswer.ts` computes security-answer HMACs with a key set in source.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Passwords are hashed with unsalted MD5, which is fast and easy to crack. The security-answer HMAC key is hardcoded in source. -->
Both primitives are weak:

- Unsalted `MD5` has no work factor, so a dump yields passwords quickly (🟠 [F-043](#f-043) — Unsalted MD5 password hashing (Unsalted `MD5` password hashing)).
- The HMAC key sits in the repository (🟡 [F-088](#f-088)).
- Session secrets draw on a non-cryptographic RNG (🟠 [F-017](#f-017)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The server fetches a requester-supplied imageUrl with no scheme, host, or private-address allowlist. Only a cookie-based login check precedes it. On fetch failure the raw URL is stored as the profile image. The extension check only chooses the file suffix. -->
`profileImageUrlUpload.ts` fetches `imageUrl` with no allowlist:

- Internal addresses are reachable (🟠 [F-047](#f-047)).
- Fetched content is stored without type checks (🟡 [F-079](#f-079)).
- A failed fetch stores the raw URL as the image.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="webhook-destination-and-payload-handling"></a>
#### 6.10.2 Webhook destination and payload handling

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - destination comes from operator configuration, but the payload leaks and is unsigned.

`SOLUTIONS_WEBHOOK` supplies the destination from the environment, and the notification is skipped when unset.

The diagram shows the webhook notification path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant App as Juice Shop Server
    participant Hook as Webhook Endpoint
    App->>App: Read SOLUTIONS_WEBHOOK
    App->>Hook: POST solved-challenge notification
    Hook-->>App: Acknowledgement
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The webhook URL comes from the operator environment variable SOLUTIONS_WEBHOOK, not from request data. The notification is skipped when the variable is unset. The payload includes the CTF flag and host details, and the request has no authentication or signature. -->
Destination control is sound, delivery is not:

- The payload carries the CTF flag and host details.
- The request has no authentication or signature.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.11 Operations Runtime and Supply Chain Controls

**Build path.** [Figure 1b](#figure-1b) shows the inputs, build systems and release artifacts that Juice Shop evidences.

Highlighted path of 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — Lockfiles disabled so every build resolves floating dependencies — .npmrc:1: npm registry → GitHub Actions → docker.io/bkimminich/juice-shop. The path stops where the evidence ends.

**Attack entries:**

- **Manipulated dependency**
  - 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — Lockfiles disabled so every build resolves floating dependencies
  - 🟡 [F-075](#f-075) — Untrusted npm Install/Postinstall Scripts Enabled
- **Manipulated CI input**
  - 🟡 [F-074](#f-074) — Heroku CLI installed by piping remote script to shell
  - 🟡 [F-076](#f-076) — GitHub Actions referenced by mutable tag or branch
  - 🟡 [F-077](#f-077) — Container base images referenced by tag instead of digest
- **Replaced release artifact**
  - 🟡 [F-073](#f-073) — Missing Container Image Signing

**Not attributable to a build step:**

- 🟡 [F-104](#f-104) — Container Runs as Root


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-026](#w-026), [W-027](#w-027), [W-037](#w-037)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing - lockfiles are disabled and dependency, install and workflow controls are incomplete.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.11.1 GitHub Actions pinned to commit SHAs](#6111-github-actions-pinned-to-commit-shas)
- [6.11.2 Dependency lockfile and install integrity](#6112-dependency-lockfile-and-install-integrity)

**Implemented controls:** GitHub Actions pinned to commit SHAs, Dependency lockfile and install integrity, Release credential handling and workflow privileges, Container image hardening and SBOM generation, CI install script suppression for lint job.

**Assessment:** Container hardening and SHA-pinned actions help, but lockfiles are off (🟠 [F-022](#f-022)), install scripts run (🟡 [F-075](#f-075)), some actions use mutable tags (🟡 [F-076](#f-076)), and workflow permissions are unset (🟡 [F-105](#f-105)). Logs and metrics are served openly.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — Lockfiles disabled so every build resolves floating dependencies
- 🟡 [F-073](#f-073) — Missing Container Image Signing

- 🟡 [F-075](#f-075) — Untrusted npm Install/Postinstall Scripts Enabled
- 🟡 [F-076](#f-076) — GitHub Actions referenced by mutable tag or branch
- 🟡 [F-077](#f-077) — Container base images referenced by tag instead of digest
- 🟡 [F-104](#f-104) — Container Runs as Root

<a id="github-actions-pinned-to-commit-shas"></a>
#### 6.11.1 GitHub Actions pinned to commit SHAs

**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - third-party actions in the inspected workflows use full commit SHAs.

`ci.yml` and `release.yml` reference third-party actions by full commit SHA.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Third-party actions in ci.yml and release.yml are pinned to full commit SHAs, limiting tag-mutation supply-chain risk. One pinned action (dawidd6/action-get-tag) is noted as archived and pending replacement. -->
Pinning is incomplete across the repository:

- Other actions still use mutable tags or branches (🟡 [F-076](#f-076)).
- `dawidd6/action-get-tag` is archived and pending replacement.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="dependency-lockfile-and-install-integrity"></a>
#### 6.11.2 Dependency lockfile and install integrity

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - lockfiles are disabled, so every build resolves floating versions.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): .npmrc; Dockerfile npm install; release.yml npm install -g -->
`.npmrc` sets `package-lock=false`. The `Dockerfile` and `release.yml` then run `npm install`, and global tools install at range or unpinned versions.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): package-lock=false disables lockfiles, so CI, release packaging and Docker builds use npm install with floating ranges. Global tools (typescript, cyclonedx-npm, grunt-cli) are installed with range or unpinned versions, giving non-reproducible builds and exposure to compromised dependency releases. -->
Builds are not reproducible:

- A compromised dependency release flows into CI, release and image builds (🟠 [F-022](#f-022)).
- Install scripts run on those builds (🟡 [F-075](#f-075)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="release-credential-handling-and-workflow-privileges"></a>
#### 6.11.3 Release credential handling and workflow privileges

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - credentials come from GitHub secrets, but token permissions are unrestricted.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): .github/workflows/release.yml secrets usage -->
`release.yml` reads registry and release credentials from GitHub secrets, triggers on tags and creates draft releases.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Registry and release credentials come from GitHub secrets, tag-triggered releases, and releases are created as drafts. In the inspected ci.yml and release.yml no explicit top-level permissions block restricts GITHUB_TOKEN scope, so the repository default applies. Other workflows were not inspected. -->
Credential sourcing is sound, privilege scoping is not:

- No top-level `permissions` block limits `GITHUB_TOKEN`, so repository defaults apply (🟡 [F-105](#f-105)).
- Other workflows were not inspected.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="container-image-hardening-and-sbom-generation"></a>
#### 6.11.4 Container image hardening and SBOM generation

**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - multi-stage distroless build, non-root user and a generated SBOM.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): Dockerfile -->
The `Dockerfile` uses a multi-stage build onto a distroless runtime, runs as user 65532, omits dev dependencies and generates a CycloneDX SBOM.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Multi-stage build with distroless runtime image, non-root user 65532, dev dependencies omitted and a CycloneDX SBOM generated. Base images are referenced by tag rather than digest. -->
Runtime hardening is solid, with reservations:

- Base images are referenced by tag, not digest (🟡 [F-077](#f-077)).
- A finding reports the container running as root (🟡 [F-104](#f-104)), which conflicts with the non-root user seen here and needs reconciling.
- Images are not signed (🟡 [F-073](#f-073)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="ci-install-script-suppression-for-lint-job"></a>
#### 6.11.5 CI install script suppression for lint job

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the lint job skips scripts, test jobs do not.

The lint job installs with `--ignore-scripts`. Test jobs run full `npm install` with lifecycle scripts.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The lint job installs with —ignore-scripts, but test jobs run full npm install including lifecycle scripts on PR-triggered runs. -->
Script suppression is partial:

- Pull-request-triggered test runs execute dependency install scripts (🟡 [F-075](#f-075)).
- A remote script piped to a shell adds a second path (🟡 [F-074](#f-074)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="rate-limiting"></a>
#### 6.11.6 Rate limiting

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - limits cover few routes and the key can be spoofed.

Rate limits apply to reset-password and the 2FA routes. Express enables trust proxy unconditionally.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Rate limits exist on reset-password and the 2FA routes. The reset-password key generator trusts the X-Forwarded-For header, which a client can spoof to evade the limit. Trust proxy is enabled unconditionally. No limits were observed on /rest/chat or other routes. -->
Throttling is narrow and bypassable:

- The reset-password key generator trusts `X-Forwarded-For`, which clients can spoof.
- No limits cover `/rest/chat` (🟠 [F-048](#f-048)) or other routes.
- 2FA throttling is weak (🟡 [F-068](#f-068)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - logs and metrics are served without authentication.

Rotating `morgan` access logs are written to a logs directory, and `/metrics` exposes runtime data.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Rotating morgan access logs are written. The logs directory is served with directory listing and no authentication. /metrics is also served without authentication. -->
Logging exists, exposure undermines it:

- The logs directory is served with listing and no authentication (🟠 [F-044](#f-044), 🟡 [F-095](#f-095)).
- `/metrics` is also open.

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="database-concurrency-and-retry-handling"></a>
#### 6.11.8 Database concurrency and retry handling

**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - busy retries and IMMEDIATE transactions reduce write conflicts.

Sequelize retries `SQLITE_BUSY` up to 5 times and runs IMMEDIATE transactions.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Retries on SQLITE_BUSY up to 5 times and uses IMMEDIATE transactions, which reduces lock contention and write-conflict failures. Query logging is disabled (logging: false), so this file produces no database audit trail. -->
This is an availability control only:

- Query logging is off (`logging: false`), so no database audit trail exists here.
- Wallet check and debit are not atomic at checkout (🟡 [F-078](#f-078)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.12 Real-time and Not Applicable Controls

**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing - Socket\.io accepts every client without authentication, and LLM actions rely on prompt text.

**Controls covered:**

- [6.12.1 LLM tool-call guardrails](#6121-llm-tool-call-guardrails)

**Implemented controls:** LLM tool-call guardrails (`generateCoupon` policy).

**Assessment:** `Socket.io` is present in the codebase. The connection handler has no authentication middleware or per-event authorization, so any client reads stored notifications and can delete entries. The chatbot guardrails sit only in prompt text.

<a id="llm-tool-call-guardrails"></a><a id="llm-tool-call-guardrails-generatecoupon-policy"></a>
#### 6.12.1 LLM tool-call guardrails

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - coupon limits exist only as prompt text and a schema description.

The `generateCoupon` tool in `routes/chat.ts` states a 10% cap and eligibility rules in the system prompt, and a zod schema describes the discount.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The 10% coupon cap and the eligibility conditions exist only as system-prompt text and a schema description. The zod schema has no max, and execute() generates a coupon for any discount value. A confidential escalation discount is embedded in the prompt. Nothing binds the action parameters to server-side policy or approval. -->
Policy is advisory to the model, not enforced:

- The schema has no `max`, and `execute()` issues a coupon for any value (🟠 [F-062](#f-062), 🟡 [F-114](#f-114), 🟡 [F-115](#f-115)).
- An escalation discount is embedded in the prompt.
- Chat inputs and stored reviews reach the model unvalidated (🟠 [F-036](#f-036), 🟡 [F-085](#f-085)).

**Relevant findings**

- None.

### 6.13 Defense-in-Depth Summary

<!-- §6.13 FORMAT — NEVER a table, at most 120 words, no file:line. Two short bullet lists: **What holds** (the individual positive controls that exist, strongest first, e.g. distroless runtime image, RS256 algorithm choice) and **Repair first** (the control-boundary repairs that would restore layered defense, each with its mitigation link [M-NNN](#m-nnn)). Do NOT emit a Markdown table — `| header |` lines under §6.13 are a contract violation. -->

**What holds**

- Distroless, non-root container image with SBOM.
- Third-party actions pinned to commit SHAs in the inspected workflows.
- `RS256` signing and a 6h token lifetime.
- Secrets for LLM and Alchemy read from environment variables.

**Repair first**

- Parameterize the login and search queries.
- Move the signing key and HMAC key out of source and pin the algorithm.
- Add server-side ownership and role checks, and stop accepting client roles.
- Re-enable lockfiles and restrict workflow permissions.

<a id="w-001"></a>
### W-001 — Application data crosses into executable code

🔴 Critical · implementation weakness · confirmed · 2 findings

An interpreter evaluates application values as code. The affected path mixes data processing with executable program construction instead of constraining callers to predefined operations.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Server-side eval of stored username in profile page

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-026](#f-026) — B2B order payload evaluated by notevil sandbox

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — replace evaluation of application values with a data parser or a fixed set of operations whose arguments remain data
- **Tactical** — ● [M-027](#m-027) — Remove eval of username, ◑ [M-004](#m-004) — Manual review: verify B2B order payload evaluated by notevil sandbox, ◕ [M-041](#m-041) — Remove dynamic evaluation of order data

<a id="w-002"></a>
### W-002 — Database access relies on concatenated queries

🔴 Critical · design weakness · confirmed · 2 findings

Database queries are assembled from application values instead of passing those values through an enforced parameterised data-access path. This leaves every call site responsible for preserving query structure.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Raw SQL string interpolation.** Several routes build database queries by concatenating request values, so each new route repeats the flaw. A point fix will not stop it; parameterized queries must be the default.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — SQL injection in product search query
- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass

**Practice sites:**

- `routes/login.ts:34`
- `routes/search.ts:23`

**Architecture evidence:** Parameterized Queries, ORM / Repository Layer, Source evidence (`routes/vulnCodeSnippet.ts:46`)

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03), [C-08](#c-08)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — provide one parameterised repository or query-builder path and prohibit application-value interpolation in database queries
- **Tactical** — ● [M-026](#m-026) — Use parameterized database queries (F-004), ● [M-028](#m-028) — Use parameterized queries

<a id="w-003"></a>
### W-003 — Privileged attributes lack an enforced write allowlist

🔴 Critical · implementation weakness · confirmed · 3 findings

Model binding accepts caller-selected attributes across a privileged write boundary. The affected binding path does not separate fields callers may change from fields reserved for server-side policy.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Mass-assignment / unscoped object binding.** Generated resources bind request bodies directly to model fields, letting callers set privileged attributes such as role.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Registration accepts privileged role field from request body

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-063](#f-063) — Registration accepts client-supplied role
- 🟡 [F-082](#f-082) — Security answer creation accepts owner id from request body
- `server.ts:402`

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — define operation-specific write schemas and assign privileged attributes exclusively through server-side policy
- **Tactical** — ● [M-029](#m-029) — Strip role from registration request bodies, ◑ [M-009](#m-009) — Manual review: verify Registration accepts client-supplied role, ◕ [M-072](#m-072) — Strip role from registration input, ◑ [M-088](#m-088) — Bind security answer owner to caller

<a id="w-004"></a>
### W-004 — Authentication relies on hand-rolled token verification

🟠 High · implementation weakness · confirmed · 6 findings

Token validation is implemented in application code rather than behind one vetted and consistently configured authentication path. Algorithm, issuer, audience, and key-handling checks can diverge between callers.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Chat identity trusted from unverified JWT decode

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist
- 🟠 [F-015](#f-015) — OAuth implicit flow without state, nonce or audience
- 🟡 [F-069](#f-069) — Token verification does not enforce expiry on role checks
- 🟡 [F-070](#f-070) — JWT verification does not pin the signing algorithm
- 🟡 [F-073](#f-073) — Missing Container Image Signing
- `routes/nftMint.ts:42`

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02), [C-03](#c-03), [C-05](#c-05), [C-07](#c-07), [C-08](#c-08)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — centralise token validation on a vetted library with pinned algorithms, issuer and audience checks, and managed signing keys
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-030](#m-030) — Verify JWT signature before trusting identity, ◑ [M-002](#m-002) — Manual review: verify JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist, ◕ [M-031](#m-031) — Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification (F-014), ◕ [M-032](#m-032) — Verify token signatures before trusting claims, ◑ [M-078](#m-078) — Validate exp, iss and aud in token verification, ◑ [M-079](#m-079) — Pin RS256 when verifying tokens, ◑ [M-018](#m-018) — Sign and verify release artifacts

<a id="w-005"></a>
### W-005 — Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation

🟠 High · design weakness · confirmed · 3 findings

Request handlers do not validate input against one enforced server-side schema or allowlist at the boundary - validation is either absent on the vulnerable parameters or limited to rejecting selected bad patterns (a blacklist). New encodings and unanticipated input forms can therefore reach downstream parsers or interpreters.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — Path Traversal
- 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — Zip slip arbitrary file write in complaint upload
- 🟡 [F-089](#f-089) — FTP file allow-list bypass

**Practice sites:**

- `routes/vulnCodeFixes.ts:81`

**Architecture evidence:** Schema Validation, Allowlist Validation, Source evidence (`server.ts:204`), Source evidence (`server.ts:246`), Source evidence (`frontend/src/app/photo-wall/photo-wall.component.ts:93`), Source evidence (`frontend/src/hacking-instructor/helpers/helpers.ts:262`) (+4 more)

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce server-side schemas and domain-specific allowlists before input reaches parsing, persistence, or command construction
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-039](#m-039) — Constrain file paths to a safe base directory (F-024), ◕ [M-040](#m-040) — Confine zip entries to the target directory, ◑ [M-095](#m-095) — Validate file name after normalization in FTP server

<a id="w-006"></a>
### W-006 — Document-database queries take their structure from application values

🟠 High · implementation weakness · confirmed · 3 findings

Document-store queries take operators or JavaScript predicates from application values instead of fixed query structures with typed values. Callers can change what a query selects, for example through injected operators or evaluated expressions.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-034](#f-034) — NoSQL JavaScript injection in order tracking \$where

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-033](#f-033) — NoSQL JavaScript injection in product
- 🟡 [F-083](#f-083) — NoSQL operator injection

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — build document queries from fixed operator structures, coerce request values to the expected scalar types, and disable server-side JavaScript evaluation in the database
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-047](#m-047) — Replace \$where string with a parameterised find, ◕ [M-046](#m-046) — Use typed query predicates instead of \$where, ◑ [M-012](#m-012) — Manual review: verify NoSQL operator injection via unvalidated review id, ◑ [M-089](#m-089) — Validate review id as a string before querying

<a id="w-007"></a>
### W-007 — Authorization is implemented route by route

🟠 High · design weakness · confirmed · 25 findings

Authorization depends on per-handler checks instead of a policy boundary that consistently enforces role, ownership, and tenant scope. New routes can bypass protection by omitting a local check.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Missing server-side authorization layer.** Ownership and role checks are written route by route, so many routes omit them. A central policy layer is needed rather than individual patches.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-003](#f-003) — Missing object ownership checks on basket resources
- 🟠 [F-050](#f-050) — Any user can edit other users' product
- 🟠 [F-055](#f-055) — Key, log and quarantine files served without authorization
- 🟠 [F-057](#f-057) — Any logged-in user can list all user accounts
- 🟠 [F-058](#f-058) — Any logged-in user can create products
- 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — Any logged-in user can list all complaints
- 🟠 [F-061](#f-061) — Unauthenticated product update
- 🟠 [F-062](#f-062) — Coupon tool discount policy enforced only in prompt
- 🟠 [F-064](#f-064) — Missing role check on user list endpoint
- 🟡 [F-106](#f-106) — Deluxe role granted without payment
- 🟡 [F-107](#f-107) — Basket item add checks first BasketId but saves last
- 🟡 [F-108](#f-108) — Product review author taken from request
- 🟡 [F-109](#f-109) — Basket items readable and deletable by any authenticated user
- 🟡 [F-113](#f-113) — Any logged-in user can delete any feedback
- 🟡 [F-116](#f-116) — Address update lacks ownership check

**Practice sites:**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — Systemic missing ownership checks
- 🟠 [F-046](#f-046) — Any authenticated user can read any user record by id
- 🟠 [F-049](#f-049) — Basket checkout lacks object ownership checks
- 🟠 [F-051](#f-051) — Recycle records readable by any caller by id
- 🟠 [F-056](#f-056) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware
- 🟡 [F-096](#f-096) — Order history filtered by lossy masked e-mail
- 🟡 [F-097](#f-097) — Unauthenticated order lookup by order id
- 🟡 [F-098](#f-098) — Order lookup by lossy vowel-masked e-mail in data
- 🟡 [F-110](#f-110) — Basket item creation can target another user's basket
- 🟡 [F-111](#f-111) — Privacy request creation not bound to authenticated user
- `routes/chat.ts:169`
- `routes/nftMint.ts:42`

**Architecture evidence:** Centralised AuthZ Policy, Role / Scope Enforcement, Ownership Check, Source evidence (`server.ts:366`), Source evidence (`server.ts:367`), Source evidence (`server.ts:371`) (+28 more)

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03), [C-07](#c-07), [C-08](#c-08)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce authorization through a shared server-side policy layer and make ownership and tenant scope mandatory inputs to data access
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-025](#m-025) — Scope basket lookup to the caller, ◕ [M-063](#m-063) — Bind review updates to the authenticated author, ◕ [M-065](#m-065) — Require an administrator role for sensitive file routes, ◕ [M-067](#m-067) — Restrict user listing to administrators, ◕ [M-068](#m-068) — Require an admin role for product creation, ◕ [M-069](#m-069) — Restrict complaint listing to the caller or admins, ◕ [M-070](#m-070) — Guard product update route with role check, ◕ [M-071](#m-071) — Enforce coupon limits in server code, ◕ [M-073](#m-073) — Restrict user listing to admin role, ◑ [M-110](#m-110) — Enforce server-side authorization, ◑ [M-111](#m-111) — Bind basket id to authenticated user, ◑ [M-112](#m-112) — Bind review author to authenticated caller, ◑ [M-113](#m-113) — Enforce basket item ownership on lookup, ◑ [M-117](#m-117) — Require admin role for feedback deletion, ◑ [M-120](#m-120) — Bind address updates to the authenticated owner, ◑ [M-001](#m-001) — Manual review: verify Systemic missing ownership checks in `routes/memory.ts:15`…, ● [M-023](#m-023) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (F-001), ◕ [M-059](#m-059) — Enforce ownership on user lookup by id, ◑ [M-006](#m-006) — Manual review: verify Basket checkout lacks object ownership checks, ◕ [M-062](#m-062) — Bind basket and wallet to the authenticated user, ◑ [M-007](#m-007) — Manual review: verify Recycle records readable by any caller by id, ◕ [M-064](#m-064) — Bind recycle lookups to the authenticated user, ◑ [M-008](#m-008) — Manual review: verify Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware, ◕ [M-066](#m-066) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint (F-056), ◑ [M-102](#m-102) — Scope order history by user id, ◑ [M-103](#m-103) — Require <a id="w-008"></a>
### W-008 — Frontend rendering lacks enforced output encoding

🟠 High · design weakness · confirmed · 8 findings

Browser rendering paths write HTML or DOM content directly without one enforced contextual-encoding or sanitisation boundary. Safety depends on each individual component using the right API.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-041](#f-041) — DOM XSS

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-037](#f-037) — Stored XSS
- 🟠 [F-038](#f-038) — Stored XSS
- 🟠 [F-039](#f-039) — Stored XSS
- 🟠 [F-040](#f-040) — DOM XSS
- 🟡 [F-072](#f-072) — Product description stored without sanitization
- 🟡 [F-086](#f-086) — Stored XSS
- 🟡 [F-087](#f-087) — XSS
- `frontend/src/app/data-export/data-export.component.ts:57`
- `frontend/src/app/data-export/data-export.component.ts:71`
- `frontend/src/app/score-board/score-board.component.ts:82`
- `frontend/src/app/about/about.component.ts:119`
- `frontend/src/app/administration/administration.component.ts:73`
- `frontend/src/app/administration/administration.component.ts:91`
- `frontend/src/app/data-export/data-export.component.ts:57`
- `frontend/src/app/last-login-ip/last-login-ip.component.ts:39`

**Architecture evidence:** Template Autoescape, Output Encoding, Content Security Policy, Source evidence (`frontend/src/app/about/about.component.ts:119`), Source evidence (`frontend/src/app/last-login-ip/last-login-ip.component.ts:39`), Source evidence (`frontend/src/app/administration/administration.component.ts:73`) (+5 more)

**Affected components:** [C-01](#c-01), [C-05](#c-05)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — use framework-safe rendering by default and isolate any required raw HTML behind one reviewed sanitisation and Trusted Types policy
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-054](#m-054) — Remove sanitizer bypass for search query, ◕ [M-050](#m-050) — Remove sanitizer bypass and encode feedback comments, ◕ [M-051](#m-051) — Render user emails as text in admin table, ◕ [M-052](#m-052) — Remove sanitizer bypass and render text safely, ◑ [M-005](#m-005) — Manual review: verify DOM XSS via trusted last login IP from token, ◕ [M-053](#m-053) — Render last login IP as escaped text, ◑ [M-010](#m-010) — Manual review: verify Product description stored without sanitization, ◑ [M-081](#m-081) — Always sanitize product description input, ◑ [M-014](#m-014) — Manual review: verify Stored XSS via product descriptions trusted as HTML, ◑ [M-092](#m-092) — Stop trusting API product description HTML, ◑ [M-015](#m-015) — Manual review: verify XSS via order id trusted as HTML in order tracking, ◑ [M-093](#m-093) — Render order number as escaped text

<a id="w-009"></a>
### W-009 — Browser scripts retain reusable session credentials

🟠 High · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

Browser code retains reusable authentication credentials in script-readable storage. Script execution in the same origin can obtain the credentials and reuse the authenticated session.

**Architectural anti-pattern - SPA without BFF.** The browser application keeps the session token in script-readable storage and no backend-for-frontend holds it server-side. Any script injection steals sessions; moving the token to a secure cookie closes it.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-002](#f-002) — Session Token in Script-Readable Storage

**Affected components:** [C-05](#c-05)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — move reusable credentials behind a server-managed session boundary and keep browser-visible state free of reusable authentication secrets
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-024](#m-024) — Store session tokens in HttpOnly, Secure cookies

<a id="w-010"></a>
### W-010 — Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store

🟠 High · design weakness · design-risk · 3 findings

Cryptographic keys, credentials, and other high-entropy secrets are embedded as literals in source or config rather than resolved at runtime from a managed secret store, so anyone with repository read access obtains reusable signing material and credentials.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Secrets hardcoded in source.** Signing keys are committed to the repository instead of a managed secret store, so anyone with source access can forge tokens.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Hardcoded JWT signing private key in source
- 🟡 [F-088](#f-088) — Hardcoded HMAC key for security answers
- 🟡 [F-101](#f-101) — Hardcoded test credentials in client bundle
- `routes/checkKeys.ts:10`

**Architecture evidence:** Managed Secret Store, Runtime Secret Injection, Source evidence (`routes/checkKeys.ts:10`), Source evidence (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`)

**Affected components:** [C-01](#c-01), [C-03](#c-03), [C-05](#c-05), [C-08](#c-08)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — move every secret to a managed secret store or injected environment configuration, rotate the exposed values, and add secret-scanning to CI
- **Tactical** — ● [M-036](#m-036) — Load JWT private key from secret store, ◑ [M-094](#m-094) — Move secrets to a managed secret store (F-088), ◑ [M-107](#m-107) — Remove embedded test credentials

<a id="w-011"></a>
### W-011 — Endpoints are reachable without enforced authentication

🟡 Medium · design weakness · confirmed · 1 finding

Sensitive API routes and real-time channels are exposed without an enforced authentication check at the endpoint boundary. Access control depends on each handler (or the caller) remembering to require a session, so an unauthenticated client can reach privileged operations directly.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟡 [F-095](#f-095) — Unauthenticated access to logs and encryption keys

**Architecture evidence:** Route Authentication Middleware, Server-Side Session Enforcement, Source evidence (`server.ts:309`), Source evidence (`server.ts:402`), Source evidence (`server.ts:404`), Source evidence (`server.ts:406`) (+4 more)

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03), [C-09](#c-09)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce authentication centrally at the routing and channel boundary so every exposed endpoint requires a verified session unless explicitly marked public
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-101](#m-101) — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint

<a id="w-017"></a>
### W-017 — Application data builds operating-system commands

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice

A command line is assembled from application values and handed to a shell. Shell syntax in those values runs as further commands with the privileges of the service.

**Practice sites:**

- `.github/workflows/update-news-www.yml:19`

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — call a fixed executable with an argument list and no shell, and accept only allowlisted argument values from callers

<a id="w-018"></a>
### W-018 — Application data crosses into template source

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

Template construction treats application values as template source. Rendering can therefore interpret data as template instructions.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-035](#f-035) — Pug template injection

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — compile fixed templates and pass application values only through their data-binding interface
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-048](#m-048) — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (F-035)

<a id="w-024"></a>
### W-024 — OAuth sign-in requests are not bound to the user's browser session

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

The client starts OAuth or OpenID Connect sign-in without a per-request state value that its callback verifies. A forged authorization response can then complete sign-in in the victim's browser with the attacker's grant.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟡 [F-067](#f-067) — OAuth implicit flow without state or nonce

**Affected components:** [C-05](#c-05)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — start every sign-in through a maintained OAuth/OIDC client that generates and verifies state (and PKCE) for each authorization request
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-076](#m-076) — Add anti-CSRF protection to state-changing requests

<a id="w-025"></a>
### W-025 — Cookie-authenticated changes lack request authenticity checks

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice

A state-changing operation accepts ambient browser credentials without an enforced check that the authenticated user initiated the request. The affected mutation path lacks a request-authenticity boundary.

**Practice sites:**

- `routes/updateUserProfile.ts:38`

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce framework CSRF protection or validated origin and anti-CSRF tokens on every mutation that accepts ambient browser credentials

<a id="w-036"></a>
### W-036 — Security-sensitive data uses weak cryptographic primitives

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 2 findings

Password, token, or integrity protection uses a weak hash, predictable random source, or insufficient work factor. The application may use a standard library, but the selected primitive does not provide the required security property.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — Non-cryptographic RNG for a secret/token
- 🟠 [F-043](#f-043) — Unsalted MD5 password hashing

**Affected components:** [C-01](#c-01), [C-08](#c-08)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — standardise on a password KDF, a CSPRNG for secrets, and modern authenticated cryptographic primitives with centrally reviewed parameters
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-003](#m-003) — Manual review: verify Non-cryptographic RNG for a secret/token, ◕ [M-034](#m-034) — Use cryptographically secure random values, ◕ [M-056](#m-056) — Hash passwords with a strong, salted algorithm

<a id="w-037"></a>
### W-037 — Build pipeline trusts mutable third-party references

🟡 Medium · design weakness · design-risk · 2 findings

CI/CD workflows resolve third-party actions and other build dependencies to mutable tags or branches instead of immutable commit digests, so a retagged or compromised upstream runs inside the pipeline with its token and secret scope.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — Lockfiles disabled so every build resolves floating dependencies — .npmrc:1
- 🟡 [F-076](#f-076) — GitHub Actions referenced by mutable tag or branch

**Architecture evidence:** Commit-SHA Action Pinning, Dependency Provenance Verification, Source evidence (`.github/workflows/image_actions.yml:33`), Source evidence (`.github/workflows/image_actions.yml:42`), Source evidence (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:188`)

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — pin every third-party action and build dependency to an immutable commit SHA (or a vetted internal mirror) and enforce SHA-pinning in CI
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-037](#m-037) — Commit lockfiles and install from them, ◕ [M-083](#m-083) — Pin third-party dependencies to immutable versions

## 8. Findings Register

Findings are grouped by severity (Critical → High → Medium → Low); within a tier they are ordered by attack vektor (Repo-Read → Internet-Anon → Internet-User → Victim-Required). Every finding card shows **Severity · Component · Location**, **Issue**, **Fix**, and **Classification** (with external CWE / OWASP links). **Evidence**, **Root cause**, violated requirements, the weakness, and the trust boundary gap appear when they apply.

**Risk Distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 5 · 🟠 High: 46 · 🟡 Medium: 52 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total findings: 103**
*This register counts every finding card below. The Management Summary's risk distribution (Total: 55) counts folded insecure-practice sites under their weakness and adds each design risk once.*
**STRIDE Coverage:** Spoofing: 14 · Tampering: 38 · Repudiation: 0 · Information Disclosure: 22 · Denial of Service: 2 · Elevation of Privilege: 27

The root causes and the fixes to do first are summarized in **Remediation Priorities** in the Management Summary; evidence-backed weaknesses are documented in the [Weakness Register](#weakness-register).

<a id="th-01"></a><a id="th-05"></a><a id="th-06"></a><a id="th-02"></a><a id="th-03"></a><a id="th-04"></a><a id="th-07"></a><a id="th-08"></a><a id="th-09"></a><a id="th-10"></a><a id="th-11"></a><a id="th-12"></a><a id="th-14"></a><a id="th-15"></a><a id="th-17"></a>

### 🔴 Critical (5)

<a id="t-001"></a><a id="f-001"></a>
#### F-001 · Systemic missing ownership checks

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical - verified attack-chain keystone in AC-T-002 (Bulk Data Exfiltration via Broken Object Authorization); see [§9](#9-abuse-cases)  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** Multiple locations (5)

**Found by:** scanners and verification ([TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01))

**Reliability:** Low. Not confirmed, this may be a false positive: UserId comes from `req.body`, but the POST `/rest/memories` route middleware (`appendUserId`) is not shown.

**Weakness:** [W-007](#w-007) - Authorization is implemented route by route

**Instances (5):** `routes/basketItems.ts`: 68; `routes/memory.ts`: 15; `routes/payment.ts`: 21, 41, 70

**Issue:** At `routes/memory.ts:15`, a signed-in attacker places another user's ID in the owner field of the request (`req.body.UserId` and the like) and the query trusts it instead of the session identity, so the attacker can read or change other users' records. At `routes/basketItems.ts:68`, a logged-in user sends PUT `/api/BasketItems/``&lt;id&gt;` for an item in another user's basket; the item is found by id only and the owner is never compared, although `isAuthorized` and `appendUserId` authenticate the caller.

The result is horizontal privilege escalation across accounts. The finding holds further places beyond these two cited locations (`location_count` is 5).

**Evidence:** ◌ ambiguous

```typescript
routes/memory.ts
  12 │     const record = {
  13 │       caption: req.body.caption,
  14 │       imagePath: 'assets/public/images/uploads/' + req.file?.filename,
→ 15 │       UserId: req.body.UserId
  16 │     }
  17 │     const memory = await MemoryModel.create(record)
  18 │     res.status(200).json({ status: 'success', data: memory })
```

**Fix:** Tie every object lookup to the requesting user's identity and reject cross-tenant references → ◑ [M-001](#m-001) — Manual review: verify Systemic missing ownership checks in `routes/memory.ts:15`… · ● [M-023](#m-023) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (🔴 [F-001](#f-001))

**Classification:** Broken Access Control · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html) · [OWASP A01:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A01_2025-Broken_Access_Control/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01) · Rules: AUTHZ-001, AUTHZ-002 · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.1](#31-systemic-missing-ownership-checks-idor-in-memory)

<a id="t-004"></a><a id="f-004"></a>
#### F-004 · SQL injection in product search query

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/search.ts:23`

**Found by:** survey, scanners and verification ([TC-INPUT-01](#tc-input-01))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-002](#w-002) - Database access relies on concatenated queries

**Trust boundary gap:** [tb-1](#tb-1) 🌐 External - external → Juice Shop Express API · Validation: The search route accepts unvalidated query input at the browser-to-API boundary and passes it to a SQL sink.

**Issue:** An unauthenticated caller sends GET `/rest/products/search?q=` with a quote-closing UNION payload. The q value is concatenated into a raw SQL string, so the caller can read other tables such as Users, including emails and password hashes, which come back in the JSON product list.

The only control is truncation of q to 200 characters, and the route has no authentication. The scanner finding at the same location also notes that interpolating request text into SQL can allow modifying rows or escalating to full database control.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  20 │   return (req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction) => {
  21 │     let criteria: any = req.query.q === 'undefined' ? '' : req.query.q ?? ''
  22 │     criteria = (criteria.length <= 200) ? criteria : criteria.substring(0, 200)
→ 23 │     models.sequelize.query(`SELECT * FROM Products WHERE ((name LIKE '%${criteria}%' OR description LIKE '%${criteria}%') AND deletedAt IS NULL) ORDER BY name`) // vuln-code-snippet vuln-line unionSqlInjectionChallenge dbSchemaChallenge
  24 │       .then(([products]: any) => {
  25 │         const dataString = JSON.stringify(products)
  26 │         if (challengeUtils.notSolved(challenges.unionSqlInjectionChallenge)) { // vuln-code-snippet hide-start
```

**Fix:** Switch all SQL execution to parameterised queries or ORM-bound parameters → ● [M-026](#m-026) — Use parameterized database queries (🔴 [F-004](#f-004))

**Classification:** Injection · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html) · [OWASP A05:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A05_2025-Injection/) · Verification items: [TC-INPUT-01](#tc-input-01) · Rules: INJ-001 · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.3](#33-sql-injection-in-product-search-query)

<a id="t-005"></a><a id="f-005"></a>
#### F-005 · Server-side eval of stored username in profile page

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/userProfile.ts:61`

**Found by:** survey, scanners and verification ([TC-INPUT-03](#tc-input-03))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-001](#w-001) - Application data crosses into executable code

**Issue:** An authenticated user stores a username of the form #{code}. When they load GET `/profile`, the handler strips the wrapper and passes the remainder to `eval()`, executing attacker JavaScript in the Node server process.

For example, a username such as #{`process.mainModule.require('child_process').execSync('id')`} would run commands as the Node process user. The scanner traced a persisted record read to this expression and notes that attacker control and configuration require verification.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  58 │         if (!code) {
  59 │           throw new Error('Username is null')
  60 │         }
→ 61 │         username = eval(code) // eslint-disable-line no-eval
  62 │       } catch (err) {
  63 │         username = '\\' + username
  64 │       }
```

**Fix:** Replace runtime code generation (eval/Function/template render) with a data-only execution path → ● [M-027](#m-027) — Remove eval of username

**Classification:** Code Execution via Unsafe Deserialization or Eval · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-95](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html) · [OWASP A08:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A08_2025-Software_or_Data_Integrity_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-INPUT-03](#tc-input-03) · Rules: INJ-NODE-007 · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.4](#34-server-side-eval-of-stored-username-in-profile-page)

<a id="t-011"></a><a id="f-011"></a>
#### F-011 · Registration accepts privileged role field from request body

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical - reaches a privileged operation on an unauthenticated endpoint; verified attack-chain keystone in AC-T-002 (Bulk Data Exfiltration via Broken Object Authorization), AC-T-004 (Privilege Escalation via Mass-Assignment on Registration), +1 more; see [§9](#9-abuse-cases)  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `server.ts:501`

**Found by:** verification ([TC-AUTHZ-03](#tc-authz-03))

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-003](#w-003) - Privileged attributes lack an enforced write allowlist

**Issue:** An anonymous caller sends POST `/api/Users` with role set to admin. The generated user resource has no field allow-list, and the role validator accepts admin, so the new account is an administrator.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  498 │   ]
  499 │
  500 │   for (const { name, exclude, model, include } of autoModels) {
→ 501 │     const resource = finale.resource({
  502 │       model,
  503 │       endpoints: [`/api/${name}s`, `/api/${name}s/:id`],
  504 │       excludeAttributes: exclude,
```

**Fix:** ● [M-029](#m-029) — Strip role from registration request bodies

**Classification:** Broken Access Control · STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege · [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html) · [OWASP A01:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A01_2025-Broken_Access_Control/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHZ-03](#tc-authz-03) · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.2](#32-registration-accepts-privileged-role-field-from-request-body)

<a id="t-010"></a><a id="f-010"></a>
#### F-010 · SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-08](#c-08) - Authentication & Session Surface  ·  **Location:** `routes/login.ts:34`

**Found by:** survey, scanners and verification ([TC-INPUT-01](#tc-input-01))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-002](#w-002) - Database access relies on concatenated queries

**Trust boundary gap:** [tb-2](#tb-2) 🌐 External - external → Authentication &amp; Session Surface · Validation: The finding's evidence line is the interface this boundary cites; CWE-89 breaks its validation condition.

**Issue:** An unauthenticated attacker posts a crafted email such as ' OR 1=1-- to `/rest/user/login` (`routes/login.ts:34`). The email is interpolated into a raw SQL query, so the password condition is bypassed and a signed JWT and basket id for the first matching user, typically the admin account, are issued.

The password is `MD5`-hashed before interpolation but the email is not neutralized. The scanner finding at the same location also notes that such interpolation can allow modifying rows or escalating to full database control.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  31 │
  32 │   return (req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction) => {
  33 │     verifyPreLoginChallenges(req) // vuln-code-snippet hide-line
→ 34 │     models.sequelize.query(`SELECT * FROM Users WHERE email = '${req.body.email || ''}' AND password = '${security.hash(req.body.password || '')}' AND deletedAt IS NULL`, { model: UserModel, plain: true }) // vuln-code-snippet vuln-line loginAdminChallenge loginBenderChallenge loginJimChallenge
  35 │       .then((authenticatedUser) => { // vuln-code-snippet neutral-line loginAdminChallenge loginBenderChallenge loginJimChallenge
  36 │         const user = utils.queryResultToJson(authenticatedUser)
  37 │         if (user.data?.id && user.data.totpSecret !== '') {
```

**Fix:** Switch all SQL execution to parameterised queries or ORM-bound parameters → ● [M-028](#m-028) — Use parameterized queries

**Classification:** Injection · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html) · [OWASP A05:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A05_2025-Injection/) · Verification items: [TC-INPUT-01](#tc-input-01) · Rules: INJ-001 · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.5](#35-sql-injection-in-login-query-enables-authentication-bypass)

### 🟠 High (46)

<a id="t-021"></a><a id="f-021"></a>
#### F-021 · Hardcoded JWT signing private key in source

**Severity:** 🟠 High - secret committed to the public source repo - extractable on clone, no prior access needed; elevated to Critical as a verified attack-chain keystone in AC-T-005 (Authentication Bypass via Exposed Secret Material); see [§9](#9-abuse-cases)  ·  **Component:** [C-08](#c-08) - Authentication & Session Surface  ·  **Location:** `lib/insecurity.ts:21`

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-010](#w-010) - Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store

**Issue:** The `RS256` private key used to sign all session tokens is embedded in source; anyone with repository or build access can forge a token for any user or role such as admin or accounting.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  19 │
  20 │ export const publicKey = fs ? fs.readFileSync('encryptionkeys/jwt.pub', 'utf8') : 'placeholder-public-key'
→ 21 │ const privateKey = '[PEM PRIVATE KEY — REDACTED] …
  22 │
  23 │ interface ResponseWithUser {
```

**Fix:** Move the credential out of source control into a secret store and rotate it → ● [M-036](#m-036) — Load JWT private key from secret store

**Classification:** Cryptographic Failures · STRIDE: Spoofing · [CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html) · [OWASP A04:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A04_2025-Cryptographic_Failures/) · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.6](#36-hardcoded-jwt-signing-private-key-in-source)

<a id="t-042"></a><a id="f-042"></a>
#### F-042 · Payment card numbers stored in cleartext

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-01](#c-01) - Sequelize application data access  ·  **Location:** `models/card.ts:38`

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-032](#w-032) - Payment card numbers stored in cleartext (`card.ts:38`)

**Issue:** The Card model persists the full card number as a plain integer column with no encryption. Anyone obtaining the SQLite file or a query result reads complete card numbers and expiry data.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  36 │       },
  37 │       fullName: DataTypes.STRING,
→ 38 │       cardNum: {
  39 │         type: DataTypes.INTEGER,
  40 │         validate: {
```

**Fix:** ◕ [M-055](#m-055) — Stop storing sensitive data in cleartext

**Classification:** Cryptographic Failures · STRIDE: Information Disclosure · [CWE-312](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/312.html) · [OWASP A04:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A04_2025-Cryptographic_Failures/)

<a id="t-002"></a><a id="f-002"></a>
#### F-002 · Session Token in Script-Readable Storage

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-05](#c-05) - frontend client application  ·  **Location:** Multiple locations (5)

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-009](#w-009) - Browser scripts retain reusable session credentials

**Instances (5):** `frontend/src/app/Services/request.interceptor.ts`: 16; `frontend/src/app/login/login.component.ts`: 101; `frontend/src/app/payment/payment.component.ts`: 232; `frontend/src/app/two-factor-auth-enter/two-factor-auth-enter.component.ts`: 53; `frontend/src/app/Services/basket.service.ts`: 64

**Issue:** The request interceptor reads the bearer token from `localStorage` on every request, so the token is stored where any injected script can read it. Combined with the XSS finding, an attacker can steal sessions.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

**Fix:** ◕ [M-024](#m-024) — Store session tokens in HttpOnly, Secure cookies

**Classification:** Insecure Client-Side Storage · STRIDE: Information Disclosure · [CWE-922](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/922.html) · [OWASP A04:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A04_2025-Cryptographic_Failures/)

<a id="t-003"></a><a id="f-003"></a>
#### F-003 · Missing object ownership checks on basket resources

**Severity:** 🟠 High - verified attack-chain keystone in AC-B-001 (User reads or changes data of another user or tenant); see [§9](#9-abuse-cases)  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** Multiple locations (6)

**Found by:** survey, scanners and verification ([TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01), [TC-AUTHZ-03](#tc-authz-03))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-007](#w-007) - Authorization is implemented route by route

**Trust boundary gap:** [tb-1](#tb-1) 🌐 External - external → Juice Shop Express API · Authorization: The finding's evidence line is the interface this boundary cites; CWE-639 breaks its authorization condition.

**Instances (6):** `routes/basket.ts`: 19; `routes/coupon.ts`: 18; `server.ts`: 426, 603, 604, 605

**Issue:** Authenticated users can reach other users' baskets by id. `retrieveBasket` (`routes/basket.ts:19`) loads a basket by `req.params.id` without comparing it to the user's basket, so ids can be enumerated to read contents.

Authentication via `isAuthorized` and `appendUserId` is present; the id comparison is only used to flag a challenge.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
routes/basket.ts
  17 │     try {
  18 │       const id = req.params.id
→ 19 │       const basket = await BasketModel.findOne({ where: { id }, include: [{ model: ProductModel, paranoid: false, as: 'Products' }] })
  20 │       /* jshint eqeqeq:false */
  21 │       challengeUtils.solveIf(challenges.basketAccessChallenge, () => {
```

**Fix:** Tie every object lookup to the requesting user's identity and reject cross-tenant references → ◕ [M-025](#m-025) — Scope basket lookup to the caller

**Classification:** Broken Access Control · STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege · [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html) · [OWASP A01:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A01_2025-Broken_Access_Control/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01), [TC-AUTHZ-03](#tc-authz-03) · Rules: AUTHZ-301

<a id="t-013"></a><a id="f-013"></a>
#### F-013 · Chat identity trusted from unverified JWT decode

**Severity:** 🟠 High - verified attack-chain contributor in AC-T-005 (Authentication Bypass via Exposed Secret Material); see [§9](#9-abuse-cases)  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/chat.ts:45`

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-004](#w-004) - Authentication relies on hand-rolled token verification

**Issue:** `getUserId` reads the bearer token and calls `security.decode`, which is `jws.decode` without signature verification, then uses `data.id` as the caller identity. An attacker forges an unsigned or self-signed token carrying another user's id and calls POST `/rest/chat`; the `getOrderById` tool then returns that user's orders (LLM06, ASI03).

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  43 │   const token = utils.jwtFrom(req)
  44 │   if (!token) return undefined
→ 45 │   const decoded = security.decode(token) as { data?: { id?: number } } | undefined
  46 │   return decoded?.data?.id
  47 │ }
```

**Fix:** Pin the signature algorithm explicitly and reject `alg:none` and unknown algorithms → ◕ [M-030](#m-030) — Verify JWT signature before trusting identity

#### F-015 · OAuth implicit flow without state, nonce or audience

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-05](#c-05) - frontend client application  ·  **Location:** `frontend/src/app/login/login.component.ts:148`

**Weakness:** [W-004](#w-004) - Authentication relies on hand-rolled token verification

**Issue:** The client starts Google sign-in with `response_type=token` and no state, nonce or PKCE, accepts any #`access_token` fragment, and logs the user in from the userinfo profile without checking the token was issued for this client. An attacker can inject a token obtained for another app, or forge the callback, to sign a victim session in as an attacker-chosen identity.

The parts disagree on throttling: one found no rate limit on the route in the served lines, another found a per-IP limit of 100 requests per 5 minutes; neither shows per-account throttling or lockout.

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/fileUpload.ts:34`

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-005](#w-005) - Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation

**Issue:** POST `/file-upload` is unauthenticated. For an uploaded .zip, `extractZipBuffer` writes each entry to uploads/complaints/ plus the attacker-controlled entry name (`routes/fileUpload.ts:34`). The upload is capped at 200000 bytes, which does not prevent the traversal.

```typescript
  36 │   }
```

**Fix:** Resolve and normalise every constructed path and reject anything that escapes the intended base directory → ◕ [M-040](#m-040) — Confine zip entries to the target directory

#### F-026 · B2B order payload evaluated by notevil sandbox

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/b2bOrder.ts:23`

**Found by:** survey and verification ([TC-INPUT-03](#tc-input-03))

**Reliability:** Low. Not confirmed, this may be a false positive: Body data reaches notevil in vm, but no sandbox escape is shown; control ineffectiveness is unproven.

**Weakness:** [W-001](#w-001) - Application data crosses into executable code

**Issue:** At `routes/b2bOrder.ts:23`, POST `/b2b/v2/orders` passes `body.orderLinesData` to the notevil evaluator inside a node:vm context. A vm context is not a security boundary and sandboxed evaluators have a history of escapes, so an authenticated caller who escapes it may execute code on the server, or may tie up the worker until the 2 second timeout.

An escape was not demonstrated in the source read.

**Evidence:** ◌ ambiguous

```typescript

  22 │         vm.createContext(sandbox)
→ 23 │         vm.runInContext('safeEval(orderLinesData)', sandbox, { timeout: 2000 })
  24 │         res.json({ cid: body.cid, orderNo: uniqueOrderNumber(), paymentDue: dateTwoWeeksFromNow() })
  25 │       } catch (err) {
```

**Fix:** Replace runtime code generation (eval/Function/template render) with a data-only execution path → ◑ [M-004](#m-004) — Manual review: verify B2B order payload evaluated by notevil sandbox · ◕ [M-041](#m-041) — Remove dynamic evaluation of order data

**Classification:** Code Execution via Unsafe Deserialization or Eval · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-94](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/94.html) · [OWASP A08:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A08_2025-Software_or_Data_Integrity_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-INPUT-03](#tc-input-03)

#### F-028 · XML parser enables external entities and DTD loading

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `lib/xml.ts:35`

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-021](#w-021) - XML parser enables external entities and DTD loading (`xml.ts:35`)

**Issue:** `parseXmlString` sets `XML_PARSE_NOENT` and `XML_PARSE_DTDLOAD` and registers filesystem input providers, so a caller-supplied XML document can read local files such as `/etc/passwd` via external entities (XXE). The upload callers were not in the slices read.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  33 │ export async function parseXmlString (data: string, timeoutMs = 2000): Promise<string> {
  34 │   const libxml2 = await loadLibxml2()
→ 35 │   const option = libxml2.ParseOption.XML_PARSE_NOENT | libxml2.ParseOption.XML_PARSE_DTDLOAD | libxml2.ParseOption.XML_PARSE_NOBLANKS | libxml2.ParseOption.XML_PARSE_NOCDATA
  36 │   const sandbox = { libxml2, data, option }
  37 │   vm.createContext(sandbox)
```

**Fix:** Disable external entity resolution on every XML parser and reject DOCTYPE declarations → ◕ [M-043](#m-043) — Disable external entities and DTD loading

**Classification:** Insecure File Handling · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-611](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/611.html) · [OWASP A06:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A06_2025-Insecure_Design/)

<a id="t-033"></a><a id="f-033"></a>
#### F-033 · NoSQL JavaScript injection in product

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/showProductReviews.ts:36`

**Found by:** survey, scanners and verification ([TC-INPUT-02](#tc-input-02))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-006](#w-006) - Document-database queries take their structure from application values

**Issue:** A caller requests GET `/rest/products/:id/reviews` with a crafted id, such as a JavaScript fragment (for example 1||true or a sleep call). At `routes/showProductReviews.ts:36`, when the `noSqlCommand` challenge flag is enabled, the id is only truncated to 40 characters and concatenated into a \$where string executed by the document database.

The scanner found request input read reaching the executable expression at line 36; it notes that attacker control, access prerequisites and active configuration require verification.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  34 │     const t0 = new Date().getTime()
  35 │
→ 36 │     db.reviewsCollection.find({ $where: 'this.product == ' + id }).then((reviews: Review[]) => {
  37 │       const t1 = new Date().getTime()
  38 │       challengeUtils.solveIf(challenges.noSqlCommandChallenge, () => { return (t1 - t0) > 2000 })
```

**Fix:** Replace string concatenation in query operators with parameter binding → ◕ [M-046](#m-046) — Use typed query predicates instead of \$where

**Classification:** Injection · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-943](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/943.html) · [OWASP A05:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A05_2025-Injection/) · Verification items: [TC-INPUT-02](#tc-input-02) · Rules: INJ-NODE-006

**Issue:** At `routes/trackOrder.ts:18`, GET `/rest/track-order/:id` places the id (read at `routes/trackOrder.ts:15`) inside a \$where JavaScript string. The character filter that strips non-word characters applies only when the `reflectedXss` challenge is disabled; otherwise the id is only truncated to 60 characters.

The scanner notes that attacker control and active configuration require verification.

```typescript
→ 18 │     db.ordersCollection.find({ $where: `this.orderId === '${id}'` }).then((order: any) => {

```

**Weakness:** [W-018](#w-018) - Application data crosses into template source

**Issue:** The username is string-replaced into the pug template source before `pug.compile`. A logged-in user who stores a username containing pug syntax or interpolation has it compiled as template code, which can inject markup or script into the profile page and, per the verification part, run on the server.

The scanner traced the persisted record read to this point and notes that attacker control requires verification.

```typescript
→ 87 │       const fn = pug.compile(template)
  88 │       const CSP = `img-src 'self' ${user?.profileImage}; script-src 'self' 'unsafe-eval'`
  89 │
```

#### F-043 · Unsalted MD5 password hashing

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-01](#c-01) - Sequelize application data access  ·  **Location:** Multiple locations (2)

**Found by:** survey, scanners and verification ([TC-AUTHN-04](#tc-authn-04))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-036](#w-036) - Security-sensitive data uses weak cryptographic primitives

**Instances (2):** `models/user.ts`: 76; `lib/insecurity.ts`: 41

**Issue:** The password setter hashes with `security.hash`, which at `lib/insecurity.ts:41` is a fast unsalted `MD5` digest with no work factor; it is used to store and compare passwords and in login, 2FA setup and disable. An attacker who obtains the SQLite file, a SQL injection dump, the user table or the hash carried in a token payload can crack passwords quickly with rainbow tables or GPU brute force and take over accounts.

No minimum password strength check is applied at the model.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified - A non-iterating hash is used for password storage.

**Fix:** Replace the broken hash with a salted password-hashing function (bcrypt/Argon2id) → ◕ [M-056](#m-056) — Hash passwords with a strong, salted algorithm

**Classification:** Cryptographic Failures · STRIDE: Information Disclosure · [CWE-916](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/916.html) · [OWASP A04:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A04_2025-Cryptographic_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHN-04](#tc-authn-04), [TC-CRYPTO-01](#tc-crypto-01) · Rules: CRYPTO-001

#### F-044 · Unauthenticated serving of keys, logs and quarantine
